lectio habeatur, hoc et ille præstabat, sibi ipse et convivis duplicem apponens mensam, ut tum corpus consueto cibo aleretur, tum anima doctrinarum voluptate oblectaretur. Quibus ille diligenter attendens animum, spiritu mentis compunctus, lacrymarum large fontes emittebat.
910 39. Ejus vero fidem erga monachos, quos præsertim sanctitas ac religio claros reddiderat, quanta fuerit, quis possit enarrare? Eorum nemo in conspectum veniebat, cui actus suos cum multis lacrymis non exponeret. Cum autem fide devotissimus ac summe orthodoxus esset, urbis omnes ecclesias pellucidis subtili stamine peplis cereorumque ac lampadum copia splendide exornabat, ac decorabat. Sed et monachis in montibus, tum nempe Olympo tum Cymina, et quem vocant Petram auream, et Barachei monte, nullo unquam tempore destitit annua stipendia mittere eorumque res curare ac eis prospicere; quique inter illos actionis cultu ac contemplatione spectabiles essent, ad se accersere, eorumque precum suffragia decerpere. Nec id modo, sed et religiosa exercitatione inclusis, et qui se angustissimis tuguriolis velut carcere abdidisent, cunctis denique monasteriis statutas annonas subministrare non cessabat. Has velut e multis paucas virtutes eleemosynas percurrimus.
40. Petro autem Bulgaro Joannes frater cum aliis Symeonis proceribus struxit insidias. Deprehensis illis, Joannes quidem verberibus emendatus in carcere includitur; reliqui acerbis suppliciis subacti sunt. Horum Romanus imperator a Petro certior factus Joannem monachum 911 (eum qui rector exstiterat) mittit, prætextu quidem redimendi captivos, re ipsa vero ut Joannem caperet et Byzantium duceret; quod et ita contigit. Quippe cum Joanne navem ingressus a Mesembria in urbem venit. Nec multo post projecto monachi habitu, cum uxoris amplexus quæreret, imperator illi ædes assignavit multaque omnino prædia contulit; uxorem quoque ex sua ipsius Armeniacorum patria ei locavit, atque in Cæsaris domo præmagnifice nuptias fecit, ductoribus sponsæ Christophoro imperatore ac Joanne monacho exrectore.
41. Mensis Martii die secunda, qui in foro, columnis illic ordine erectis, Cosmites impositus erat, decidit ac viros sex occidit. Magnum quoque ac formidabile in fori porticu excitatum incendium, haud procul a templo sanctæ Deiparæ, adeo ut pars illa fori quam cerarii et quam pellitiarii artifices occupant, ad Psichas usque igni absumpta sit.
42. Mortuus autem est Christophorus imperator mense Augusto, indictione quarta, Romano

αὐτὴν τοῦ πατρὸς, ὡς ἤδη εἰς γῆρας αὐτοῦ ἐληλακότος καὶ τῶν υἱῶν αὐτοῦ ἔτι νηπίων ὄντων. Καὶ ἐτέθη τὸ λείψανον αὐτοῦ ἐν τῇ ῥηθείσῃ μονῇ τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτοῦ.
μγ΄. Κατήγαγον δὲ καὶ Τρύφωνα πατριάρχην Αὐγούστῳ μηνὶ, τὸν ῥητὸν χρόνον τελέσαντα, ἰνδικτιῶνος γ΄. Καὶ ἀπελθὼν ἐν τῇ ἰδίᾳ μονῇ τελευτᾷ, χηρευσάσης τῆς Ἐκκλησίας χρόνον ἕνα καὶ μῆνας ε΄ διὰ τὸ τῆς ἡλικίας ἀτελὲς Θεοφυλάκτου υἱοῦ Ῥωμανοῦ βασιλέως· αὐτὸν γὰρ, ὡς εἴρηται, ἔμελλε πατριάρχην ἀποκαθιστᾶν.
μδ΄. Βασίλειος δέ τις Μακεδὼν πλάνος, Κωνσταντῖνον Δοῦκα ἑαυτὸν εἶναι ἐπιφημίσας, πολλοὺς μεθ΄ ἑαυτοῦ συνεπήγετο. Οὗτος κρατηθεὶς ὑπὸ Ἐλεφαντίνου τουρμάρχου ἐκ τοῦ Ὀψικίου ὄντος ἤχθη ἐν Κωνσταντινουπόλει, καὶ ὑπὸ τοῦ ἐπάρχου τῆς μιᾶς ἀλλοτριοῦται χειρός. Εἶτα καιροῦ λαβόμενος καταλαμβάνει πάλιν τὸ Ὀψίκιον, καὶ χεῖρα χαλκῆν ἀντὶ τῆς κοπείσης προσαρμοσάμενος καὶ σπάθην ὑπερμεγέθη κατασκευασάμενος ᾤχετο πλανῶν αὖθις τῶν ἀγυτευόντων πολλοὺς, ὡς αὐτὸς εἴη Κωνσταντῖνος ὁ τοῦ Δουκός· οὓς καὶ συνεφελκόμενος μεγάλην κατὰ Ῥωμανίας ἀνταρσίαν κινεῖ. Καὶ φρούριον κατασχὼν ὃ Πλατεῖα πέτρα κατονομάζεται, πᾶν εἶδος ἐδωδίμων ἐν τούτῳ ἀπέθετο· ἀφ΄ οὗ ἐξορμῶν ἐληλάτει καὶ τοὺς παρατυχόντας προενόμευεν. Ἀποστείλας οὖν ὁ βασιλεὺς στρατὸν κατ΄ αὐτοῦ αὐτόν τε συνέλαβεν καὶ τοὺς σὺν αὐτῷ. Ὃν καὶ ἀγαγόντες ἐν τῇ πόλει ἀνέκριναν τε καὶ πολλὰς πληγὰς ἐπετίθεσαν, τοῦ τοὺς στασιώτας, εἴ τινες εἶεν, εἰπεῖν. Ὁ δὲ πολλοὺς τῶν ἐν τέλει ἐσυκοφάντει ψευδῶς ὡς συνὄντας αὐτῷ. Μηδὲν δὲ ἀληθῆ κατ΄ αὐτῶν λέγειν ἐξελεγχθεὶς ἐν τοῖς Ἀμαστριαναῦ πυρὶ παρανάλωμα γίνεται.
με΄. Χειροτονεῖται δὲ πατριάρχης ὁ πολλάκις ῥηθεὶς τοῦ βασιλέως υἱὸς Θεοφύλακτος, Φεβρουαρίου β΄, ἰνδικτιῶνος ϛ΄, τοποτηρητῶν [P. 588] ἐκ Ῥώμης ἐλθόντων καὶ τόμον συνοδικὸν ἐπιφερομένων περὶ τῆς αὐτοῦ χειροτονίας διαγορεύοντα· οἳ καὶ τῷ πατριαρχικῷ θρόνῳ τοῦτον ἐνίδουσαν.
μϛ΄. Ἡ δὲ εἰρημένη του βασιλέως Ῥωμανοῦ ἐγγόνη Μαρία, ἡ γυνὴ Πέτρου βασιλέως Βουλγαρίας, ἐν τῇ πόλει ἐσῆλθε τοῦ ἴδιον πατέρα καὶ τὸν πάππον αὐτῆς ἐπισκέψασθαι. Τελευταῖον δὲ μετὰ παίδων ἐσῆλθε τριῶν, ἤδη τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτῆς Χριστοφόρου τελευτήσαντος. Πλοῦτον οὖν παρὰ τοῦ πάππου λαβοῦσα πολὺν καὶ ἔντιμον ἐντίμως ὑπέστρεψε.
μζ΄. Ῥωμανὸς δὲ ὁ βασιλεὺς τῷ υἱῷ Στεφάνῳ γυναῖκα ἠγάγετο, θυγατέρα οὖσαν Γαβριηλίου, ἐγγόνην δὲ Κατακύλα, Ἄνναν ὀνόματι. Ἅμα δὲ τῷ νυμφικῷ στεφάνῳ καὶ τῆς βασιλείας αὐτῷ στέφανος ἐπετέθη.

Sed et Tryphonem patriarcham mense Augusto, indictione 912 tertia, expleto jam statuto tempore, gradu moverunt; abiensque in sua monasteria fatis concedit, Ecclesia pastore vidua manente annum unum menses quinque, quo.l Theophylactus Romani imperatoris filius nondum matura satis ætate esset : ipsum enim pater, uti dictum est, constituerat patriarcham præficere.
44. Basilius quidam Macedo impostor, Constantinum Ducem se esse jactans, multos sibi adjunxit. Hic ab Elephantino tribuno Opsiciano captus inque urbem adductus a præfecto altera manuum truncatus est Postmodum vero occasionem nactus ad Opsicium redit; æreamque sibi manum aptans ejus loco quæ excisa erat, ingentemque gladium fabricans, multos iterum circumforaneos ac nebulones seducens sic vagabatur, velut qui Constantinus Ducis filius esset. Iterum itaque contracta manu magnam adversus rem Romanam conflat rebellionem; oppidoque munito potitus, cui nomen Lata petra, omnisque generis in eo annona reposita, inde erumpens cir cum vicinam regionem populabatur lateque prædas agebat. Missis itaque imperator adversus eum copiis tum ipsum cepit, tum qui illi socii erant. Inductum in urbem judicio sistunt; atque ad quæstiones adhibito, et ut socios prodat, multas plagas imponunt : qui multos procerum, quasi sibi adhæsissent, per calumniam defert. Convictu; vero nihil eis veri criminis objicere, ad Amastriani igni combustus est.
913 45. Quem vero sæpius dixi, imperatoris filius Theophylactus patriarcha ordinatur, Februarii mensis die secunda, indictione sexta, cum adessent ex Roma vicarii, tomumque synodalem de illius tractantem ordinatione inferrent; qui et in patriarchali sede eum collocavere.
46. Quin dicta quoque Romani imperatoris neptis Maria, Petri Bulgariæ principis uxor, in urbem ingressa est patrem avumque invisura. Postremo tandem venit tres secum liberos adducens, cum jam Christophorus, ejus pater vivis exemptus esset. Multis itaque præclarisque avi munificentia donata divitiis honorifice, domum reversa est.
47. Romanus vèro imperator Stephano filio uxorem locat Gabrielis filiam, Catacylæ neptim, Annam nomine; ac cum nuptiali serto Augustalis etiam corona illi imposita est.
48. Contigit vero prima Turcorum adversus Romanos expeditio mense Aprili, indictione


[975] {#pg.109.975}
septima. Hi ad ùrbem usque excurrentes cunctos per Thraciam obvios præda abegere. Missus itaque Theophanes patricius protovestiarius imperiique administer ac princeps senatus, ut pacem cum eis componeret captivosque reciperet. Porro Theophanes 914 admiranda prudentia ac sagacitate illis usus cuncta ex voto confecit, multam ex illis eo nomine laudem ac admirationem consecutus. Quando et Romanus imperator, quarta magnificentia et humanitate præstaret, manifestum fecit, nullis sumptibus parcens, solum ut liberarentur captivi.
49. Locavit vero Romanus imperator Constantino filiorum natu minimo uxorem Helenam, Adriani patricii filiam, stirpis Armeniacæ; qua et defuncta mensis Februarii die altera alteram ei Theophanonem nomine nuptam tradidit.
50. Junii mensis undecima, indictione quinta decima, Russi instructa classe ad decem navium millia adversus Byzantium navigarunt. Missus vero est cum triremibus et celocibus, quotquot in urbe exstabant, Theophanes patricius subadjuva et protovestiarius; instructaque et parata classe, jejunii ac lacrymarum apprime præsidio munitus, Russos operiebatur, navale cum eis certamen commissurus. Cumque illi jam venissent ac Phare propinquassent, ipse ad Euxini fauces stationem habens, in loco quem Hierum vocant (ac si templum dicas) repente in eos impressionem fecit. Primus itaque sua ipse occurrens celoce cum Russicarum navium aciem dissolvit, tum plerasque præparato igne 915 Græco incendit, reliquas in fugam vertit. Quem pone secutæ naves reliquæ ac triremes hostium penitus aciem fudere; ac plures quidem navium cum vectoribus profundo mersere, multos straverunt, plurimos vivos ceperunt. Quæ cladi superstites fuere reliquiæ, ad Orientalem plagam, ad locum Sgora dictum trajecerunt. Ibi Bardas quoque patricius missus cum equitum delecta manu ad hostium iter terra intercipiendum, in eorum cohortem satis numerosam, ad pabulandum aliaque necessaria ad Bithyniæ partes missam, incidit, ingentique eam strage fudit et internecione delevit. Sed et Joannes magister Crocoas ac legionum domesticus cum Asiatico omni exercitu præsto fuit, multosque Russorum a reliquo agmine alios alio divulsos offendens sustulit, ita sane ut ejus aggressionis metu in suis se navibus adunata classe continerent, nec usquam excurrere auderent.
51. Ili porro ingentia maximæ mala perpetraverunt, cum nedum illos Romanæ copiæ junxissent. Nam Stenum omne, quod vocant, igne vastarant. Ex illis quos captivos ceperunt, alios


[976] {#pg.109.976}
καὶ καταδραμόντες μέχρι τῆς πόλεως ἐλήϊσαντο πᾶσαν Θραῲων ψυχήν. Ἀπεστάλη οὖν ὁ πατρίκιος Θεοφάνης ὁ πρωτοβεστιάριος καὶ παραδυναστεύων μετ᾿ αὐτῶν ποιῆσαι ἀλλάγιον, ὃς καὶ θαυμασίως καὶ συνετῶς αὐτοὺς μετελθὼν ὅσαπερ ἠβούλετο κατεπράξατο, πολλὰ παρ᾿ αὐτῶν ἐπὶ τῇ φρονήσει καὶ εὐβουλίᾳ ἐπαινεθεὶς τε καὶ θαυμασθείς· ὅτε καὶ τὸ μεγαλόψυχον αὐτοῦ καὶ φιλάνθρωπον ὁ βασιλεὺς Ῥωμανὸς ἐπεδείξατο, μηδενὸς φεισάμενος χρήματος πρὸς τὴν τῶν αἰχμαλώτων ἀνάῤῥυσιν.
μθ΄. Ἠγάγετο δὲ γυναῖκα ὁ βασιλεὺς Ῥωμανὸς τῷ τελευταίῳ αὐτοῦ υἱῷ Κωνσταντίνῳ ἐκ γένους τῶν Ἀρμενιακῶν, τοὔνομα Ἑλένην, τοῦ πατρικίου Ἀδριανοῦ θυγατέρα· ἧς καὶ τελευτησάσης Φεβρουαρίῳ μηνί, ἰνδικτιῶνος β΄, ἑτέραν τούτῳ συνἐζευξε.
ν΄. Ἰουνίῳ δὲ μηνὶ ια΄, ἰνδικτιῶνος ιε΄, κατέπλευσαν οἱ Ῥῶς κατὰ Κωνσταντινουπόλεως μετὰ πλοίων χιλιάδων δέκα. Ἀπεστάλη δὲ κατ᾿ αὐτῶν μετὰ τριηρῶν καὶ δρομώνων, ὅσα καὶ ἔτυχον ἐν τῇ πόλει, ὁ πατρίκιος Θεοφάνης ὁ παραδυναστεύων καὶ πρωτοβεστιάριος· καὶ τὸν τε στόλον προευτρεπίσας τε καὶ ἑτοιμασάμενος, καὶ νηστείᾳ καὶ δάκρυσιν ἑαυτὸν κατοχυρώσας ὡς μάλιστα, τοὺς Ῥῶς προσεδέχετο, καταναυμαχῆσαι αὐτοὺς μέλλων. Ἐπεὶ δὲ ἐκεῖνοι κατέλαβον καὶ πλησίον τοῦ Φάρου ἐγένοντο, οὗτος πρὸς τῷ τοῦ Εὐξείνου πόντου στόματι παρεδρεύων ἐν τῷ Ἱερῷ λεγομένῳ ἀθρόον τούτοις ἐπέθετο. Καὶ δὴ πρῶτος τῷ οἰκείῳ δρόμωνι διεκπλεύσας [P. 589] τήν τε σύνταξιν τῶν Ῥωσιακῶν πλοίων διέλυσε καὶ τῷ ἐσκευασμένῳ πυρὶ πλεῖστα κατέφλεξεν, τὰ δὲ λοιπὰ ἐτρέψατο εἰς φυγὴν, ὡς ἀκολούθως καὶ οἱ λοιποὶ δρόμωνες καὶ αἱ τριήρεις ἐπεκδραμοῦσαι τελείαν εἰργάσαντο τὴν τροπήν, καὶ πολλὰ μὲν πλοῖα κατέδυσαν αὔτανδρα, πολλοὺς δὲ κατέτρωσαν, πλείστους δὲ ζῶντας συνέλαβον. Οἱ περιλειφθέντες οὖν εἰς τὸ τῆς Ἀνατολῆς μέρος εἰς τὰ Σγῦρα 66 λεγόμενα καταπλέουσιν. Ἀπεστάλη δὲ τότε καὶ Βάρδας ὁ Φωκᾶς διὰ γῆς μετὰ ἱππέων ἐγκρίτων παρατρέχειν αὐτούς. Καὶ δὴ τούτων σύνταγμα ἱκανὸν ἀποστειλάντων πρὸς τὰ τῆς Βιθυνίας μέρη ὥστε τὰ πρὸς τροφὴν καὶ τὴν ἄλλην χρείαν αὐτοῖς πορίσασθαι, περιτυχὼν τῷ τοιούτῳ συντάγματι ὁ εἰρημένος Βάρδας ὁ Φωκᾶς κακῶς τούτους διέθηκεν, τρέψάμενος καὶ κατασφάξας αὐτούς. Κατῆλθε δὲ τηνικαῦτα καὶ Ἰωάννης μάγιστρος καὶ δόμεστικος τῶν σχολῶν ὁ Κροκόας 67 μετὰ παντὸς τοῦ τῆς Ἀνατολῆς στρατεύματος, καὶ τούτων πολλοὺς διέφθειρεν, ἀποσπάδας τῇδε κἀκεῖσε καταλαμβάνων, ὥστε συσταλέντας αὐτοὺς δέει τῆς αὐτοῦ ἐπιθέσεως μένειν ἀθρόως ἐπὶ τὰ οἰκεῖα πλοῖα καὶ μηδαμοῦ διατρέχειν κατατολμᾶν.
να΄. Πολλὰ δὲ μέγιστα κακὰ οὗτοι διεπράξαντο πρὸ τοῦ τὸ Ῥωμαϊκὸν στράτευμα κατελθεῖν. Τό τε γὰρ Στενὸν λεγόμενον ἅπαν ἐνέπρησαν, καὶ οὓς συνελάμβανον αἰχμαλώτους τοὺς μὲν ἀνεσταύ-


[977] {#pg.109.977}
ρουν, τοὺς δὲ τῇ γῇ προσεπαττάλευον, τοὺς δὲ ὥσπερ σκοποὺς ἱστῶντες βέλεσι κατετόξευον· ἵσους δὲ τοῦ ἱερατικοῦ κλήρου συνελάμβανον, ὄπισθεν τὰς χεῖρας δεσμοῦντες ἥλοις ἰσιδήροις κατὰ μέσης τῆς κεφαλῆς αὐτῶν κατεπήγνυον· πολλοὺς τε ἁγίους ναοὺς τῷ πυρὶ παραδεδώκασι. Χειμῶνος δὲ ἤδη ἐνισταμένου, καὶ τροφῆς ἀποροῦντες, τό τε ἐπελθὸν στράτευμα δεδιότες, τάς τε ναυμαχοῦντας τριήρεις, ἐβουλεύσαντο τὰ οἰκεῖα καταλαβεῖν. Καὶ δὴ λαβεῖν τὸν στόλον σπουδάζοντες, Σεπτεμβρίῳ μηνί, ιε´ ἰδικτιῶνος ἀντιπλεῦσαι ὡρμηκότες ἐπὶ τὰ Θρακῷα μέρη παρὰ Θεοφάνους, πατρικίου ὑπηντήθησαν· οὐ γὰρ ἔλαθον τὴν αὐτοῦ ἐγρηγορωτάτην καὶ γενναίαν ψυχήν. Εὐθὺς οὖν δευτέρα ναυμαχία συνάπτεται, καὶ πλεῖστα πλοῖα ἐπόντωσεν καὶ πολλοὺς τούτων ἀπέκτεινεν ὁ εἰρημένος ἀνήρ. Ὀλίγοι δὲ μετὰ τῶν πλοίων περισωθέντες καὶ ἐν τῇ τῆς Κοίλης περιπεσόντες ἀκτῇ νυκτὸς ἐπελθούσης ἔφυγον. Θεοφάνης δὲ πατρίκιος μετὰ νίκης λαμπρᾶς καὶ τῶν μεγίστων ὑπέστρεψεν τροπαίων, καὶ ἐντίμως καὶ μεγαλοπρεπῶς ὑπεδέχθη καὶ παρακοιμώμενος ἐτιμήθη.
[Ρ. 590] νβ´. Ἐπεὶ δὲ ὁ ῥηθεὶς Ἰωάννης μάγιστρος ὁ Κροκόας ἄριστος τὶς ἐγένετο τὰ πολεμικά, πολλά τε καὶ μεγάλα ἀνέστησε τρόπαια, καὶ τὰ Ῥωμαϊκὰ ἐπλάτυνεν ὅρια, πλείστας τε πόλεις ἐπόρθησεν τῶν Ἀγαρηνῶν διὰ δὲ τὸ περιφανὲς τῆς τοῦ ἀνδρὸς ἀρετῆς ἠβουλήθη ὁ βασιλεὺς Ῥωμανὸς εἰς τὸν ἑαυτοῦ ἔγγονον Ῥωμανόν, τὸν ἐκ τοῦ Κωνσταντίνου τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ, τὴν τούτου θυγατέρα ἀναλαβεῖν. Φθόνου δὲ αὐτῷ τῆς τοιαύτης ἕνεκεν ὑποθέσεως παρὰ τῶν λοιπῶν βασιλέων κινηθέντος ἐκπίπτει τῆς ἀρχῆς, δομέστικος δὲ ἀντικαθίσταται ὁ πατρίκιος Πανθήριος, συγγενὴς ὢν τοῦ βασιλέως Ῥωμανοῦ.
νγ´. Ἰδικτιῶνος α´, Ἀπρίλλῳ μηνί, ἐπῆλθον πάλιν οἱ Τοῦρκοι μετὰ πλείστης δυνάμεως. Ὁ δὲ πατρίκιος Θεοφάνης ὁ παρακοιμώμενος ἐξελθὼν σπονδὰς εἰρηνικὰς ἐποίησε μετ᾿ αὐτῶν, ὁμήρους τῶν ἐμφανῶν ἀναλαβόμενος ἐξ οὗ καὶ συνέβη ἐπὶ χρόνοις ε´ τὴν εἰρήνην διαφυλαχθῆναι.
νδ´. Δευτέρας δὲ ἰδικτιῶνος ὁ βασιλεὺς Ῥωμανὸς Πασχάλιον πρωτοσπαθάριον καὶ στρατηγὸν Λογγιβαρδίας πρὸς τὸν ῥῆγα Φραγγίας Οὔγουνα 68 ἐξἀπέστειλε, τὴν αὐτοῦ θυγατέρα ἐπιζητῶν εἰς νύμφην Ῥωμανῷ τῷ υἱῷ τοῦ γαμβροῦ αὐτοῦ Κωνσταντίνου· ἣν καὶ ἀναλαβὼν ὁ εἰρημένος Πασχάλιος μετὰ πλούτου πολλοῦ ἐν τῇ πόλει ἀνήγαγεν. Γέγονε δὲ ὁ γάμος Σεπτεμβρίῳ μηνί ἰδικτιῶνος γ´. Ζήσασα ἔτη ε´, ἐτελεύτησεν ἐν ταῖς ἡμέραις τῆς αὐτοκρατορίας Κωνσταντίνου τοῦ αὐτῆς πενθεροῦ.
νε´. Ἀνέμου δὲ βιαίου σφόδρα καταπνεύσαντος Δεκεμβρίῳ μηνί, οἱ λεγόμενοι ἐν τῷ ἱππικῷ δῆμοι


[978] {#pg.109.978}
terræ configebant, alios velut ad metam erectos jaculorum imbre impetebant. Quotquot 916 vero sacerdotalis ac clericalis militiæ deprehendebant, iis retro vinetis manibus per medium caput clavos ferreos adigebant. Multas sacrarum ædium subjecta flamina combusserunt. Ingruente vero jam hieme, et cum eos necessarius commeatus deficeret, dum terra exercitus in se impetum ac navale prælium timent, domum reverti constituerunt. Ac sane classem latere dum student, mense Septembre, indictione quinta decima, ad Thraciæ portus transfretare aggressi, quem dicebam, Theophanem patricium obviam habuere. Neque enim viri solertissimum ac pervigilem animum unaque fortissimum fefellerunt. Mox igitur navalis denuo pugna committitur; plurimæque hostium submersæ naves, multi occisi, ejus Marte, quem dicebam, viri. Pauci cum navibus cladi subducti inque Cœles asperum littus evecti ingruente nocte fuga evaserunt. Theophanes vero patricius cum insigni victoria maximisque tropæis reversus honorifice ac magnifice suscipitur, ac accubitoris dignitate ornatur.
52. Quia vero dictus Joannes magister Crocoas præstantissima virtute bello fulserat, multaque ac magna erexerat tropæa, atque Romani imperii fines late protulerat, plurimasque Agarenorum urbes everterat, quodque viri illustrissima virtus esset, cogitaverat Romanus imperator 917 ut ejus filium Romano nepoti ex Constantino filio uxorem ascisceret. Conflata vero hinc viro reliquorum Augustorum invidia, officio movetur. Hujus porro loco domesticus constituitur Pantherius patricius, Romani imperatoris propinquus.
53. Indictione prima, mense Aprili, rursus Turci cum ingentibus copiis Romanæ ditionis fines invaserunt. Egressus vero Theophanes patricius accubitor pacis cum eis fœdus percussit acceptis obsidibus ex illustrioribus. Hincque adeo contigit in annos quinque pacem induciasque servari.
54. Secunda porro indictione Romanus imperator Paschalium protospatharium ac Longobardiæ ducem ad Hugonem misit Franciæ regem, ejus filiam Romano Constantini generi sui filio sponsam exquirens; quam et assumens, quem dixi, Paschalius cum multa suppelectile atque opibus in urbem induxit. Celebratæ nuptiæ, mense Septembri, indictione tertia. Vixit cum viro annos quinque; tumque vivis exempta est, cum Constantinus illius socer ipse summus imperator esset unusque rerum potiretur.
55. Mense autem Decembri, valido concitato vento atque turbine, 918 qui in foro ex adverso


[979] {#pg.109.979}
κατέπεσον, οἱ ἀπεναντίας τοῦ βασιλικοῦ θρόνου ἐτύγχανον, καὶ συνέτριψαν τά τε ὑποκάτωθεν αὐτῶν βάθρα καὶ τὰ στήθεα λεγόμενα. Χρόνου δὲ περικυκλεύσαντος τῷ αὐτῷ μηνὶ τὸν βασιλέα Ῥωμανὸν οἱ υἱοὶ αὐτοῦ τοῦ παλατίου κατήγαγον. νσ΄. Τῆς δὲ πόλεως Ἐδέσης, ἐν ᾗ τὸ τοῦ Χριστοῦ τίμιον ἐκμαγεῖον ἀπέκειτο, παρὰ τοῦ Ῥωμαϊκοῦ πολιορκουμένης στρατεύματος καὶ εἰς ἀνάγκην μεγίστην περισταμένης, ἀπέστειλαν οἱ ταύτης οἰκήτορες πρὸς τὸν βασιλέα Ῥωμανὸν διαπρεσβευόμενοι τὴν πολιορκίαν λυθῆναι, τὸ τοῦ Χριστοῦ ἅγιον ἐκμαγεῖον παρέχειν ἐπαγγειλάμενοι. Ὑπὲρ τῆς τοιαύτης δὲ χάριτος δεσμίους ἑθήσαντο τῶν ἐμφανῶν ἀπολαβεῖν, χρυσόβουλλά τε λαβεῖν ὥστε τὴν τούτων χώραν μηκέτι παρὰ τοῦ Ῥωμαίων στρατοῦ ληΐζεσθαι· ὃ δὴ [P. 591] καὶ γέγονεν. Τοῦ δὲ ἁγίου ἐκμαγείου ἀποσταλέντος καὶ ἤδη τῇ Κωνσταντινουπόλει πλησιάσαντος, Θεοφάνης ὁ πατρίκιος καὶ παρακοιμώμενος ἐν τῷ ποταμῷ Σαγγάρῳ ἐξελθὼν ὑπήντησεν αὐτῷ μετὰ φωταγωγίας καὶ τῆς δέουσης τιμῆς καὶ ὑμνῳδίας. Καὶ τῇ ιε΄ τοῦ Αὐγούστου μηνὸς σὺν αὐτῷ ἐν τῇ πόλει εἰσῆλθον, τοῦ βασιλέως ἐν Βλαχέρναις ὄντος κἀκεῖ αὐτὸ προσκυνήσαντος. Τῇ ἐπαύριον ἐξῆλθον ἐν τῇ Χρυσῇ πόρτῃ οἵ τε βασιλέως δύο υἱοὶ Στέφανός τε καὶ Κωνσταντῖνος καὶ ὁ γαμβρὸς αὐτοῦ Κωνσταντῖνος σὺν τῷ πατριάρχῃ Θεοφυλάκτῳ· καὶ μετὰ τῆς δέουσης τοῦτο ἀναλαβόντες τιμῆς, τῆς συγκλήτου πάσης προπορευομένης καὶ φωταγωγίας μεγίστης προαγούσης, μέχρι τοῦ ἁγίου ναοῦ τῆς Ἁγίας τοῦ Θεοῦ Σοφίας πεζῇ διεκόμισαν, καὶ προσκυνηθὲν ἐκεῖσε ἐν τῷ παλατίῳ ἀνήγαγον. νζ΄. Ἐν ταύταις δὲ ταῖς ἡμέραις Ἀρμένιόν τι τέρας τῇ πόλει ἐπεδήμησεν, παῖδες συμφυεῖς ἄῤῥενες ἐκ μιᾶς προελθόντες γαστρός, σῶοι μὲν καὶ ἀρτιοι πάντα τὰ μέλη τοῦ σώματος, ἀπὸ δὲ τοῦ στόματος τῆς γαστρὸς καὶ μέχρι τῶν ὑπογαστρίων συμπεφυκότες καὶ ἀλλήλοις ὑπάρχοντες ἀντιπρόσωποι. Οἳ ἐπὶ πλεῖστον τῇ πόλει ἐνδιατρίψαντες, καὶ ὑπὸ πάντων ὡς ἐξαίσιον τι τέρας ὁρώμενοι, τῆς πόλεως ὡς πονηρός τις οἰωνὸς ἐξηλάθησαν. Ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς μονοκρατορίας Κωνσταντίνου πάλιν εἰσῆλθον· ἐπεὶ δὲ ὁ ἕτερος αὐτῶν ἐτεθνήκει, ἰατροὶ τινες ἔμπειροι τὸ συγκεκολλημένον μέρος διέτεμον εὐφυῶς, ἐλπίδι τοῦ ἕτερον ζήσεσθαι, ὃς τρεῖς ἡμέρας ἐπιδιοὺς ἐτελεύτησεν. νη΄. Ὁ δὲ Ῥωμανὸς ὁ βασιλεύς, ὡς ἀνωτέρω εἴρηται, ἐν πᾶσιν μοναχοῖς πίστιν ἐκέκτητο ἄμετρον, διαφερόντως δὲ ἐτίμα καὶ ὑπερεσέβετο Σέργιον τὸν ἐν μονασταῖς διαλάμποντα, ὃς ἀδελφὸς μὲν τοῦ μαγίστρου Κοσμᾶ, ἀνεψιὸς δὲ τοῦ πατριάρχου Φωτίου· ὃν πλέον τῆς σαρκικῆς εὐσυγγενείας 69 ἢ κατὰ ψυχὴν ἐτίμα εὐγένεια. Ἀρετῆς γὰρ εἰς ἄκρον καὶ γνώσεως ἤλασεν, ὡς δυσχερὲς εἶναι διακρίνειν ποτέρῳ μᾶλλον ἐπλεονέκτει· οὕτως εἰς ἄκρον ἑκατέραν ἐξήσκησεν. Ἐπήνθει τε αὐτῷ πρὸς τοῖς ἄλλοις καὶ τὸ τῆς διακρίσεως χάρισμα καὶ τὸ χάριεν τοῦ


[980] {#pg.109.980}
regii solii erecti sunt, sic dicti, demi corruerunt, subjectosque gradus et marmora et intercolumnia contriverunt. Anni autem vertente circulo, eodem ipso mense, Romanum imperatorem ejus fili aula dejecerunt. 56. Edessenâ autem civitate, in qua venerandi Christi effigies linteo expressa erat, a Romano exercitu obsessa et ad extremas angustias redacta miserunt oppidani ad Romanum imperatorem oratores rogantes solvi obsidionem, ejus muneris gratiæ sacram Christi sic expressam effigiem se daturos polliciti; hujusque ipsius muneris vicem ex suis vinctos illustriores recipere petebant, bullaque aurea eam eis præstari fidem ut non amplius Romanæ copiæ Edessenam regionem devastarent. Quod et factum est. Missa itaque sacra effigie ac sudario, jamque haud procul Byzantio, Theophanes patricius et accubitor ad Sagarim fluvium egressus cum cereorum splendido apparatu congruoque cultu et canticis illi obviam processit; mensisque Augusti quinta decima cum illo in urbem intravit, imperatore in Blachernis constituto, ubi et effigiem devote adoravit. Postridie vero ad Auream portam exierunt tum imperatoris duo filii, Stephanus et Constantinus, tum illius gener Constantinus 919 cum Theophylacto patriarcha, debitoque honore susceptum sacrum pignus, universo senatu præeunte maximaque cereorum atque lampadum silva prælucente, adusque sanctæ Dei sapientiæ templum pedibus detulere, atque illic adoratione mysticoque cultu acceptum in palatium intulere. 57. His vero diebus monstrum quoddam ac portentum Armeniacum in urbem invectum est, pueri scilicet duo cohærentes, uno utero effusi, aliis membris omnibus corporis integri, a ventriculi tamen ore et intra pubem inter se concreti, atque vultibus obversis. Hi diu in urbe versati; ac cum omnes cum immape quoddam portentum oculis usurparent, Romanus imperator velut malum quoddam omen ea expulit. Cum autem solus jam Constantinus rerum potiretur, iterum ingressi sunt. Uno autem ex eis mortuo, periti quidam medici cohærescentem emortuam partem subtili arte pulvere, fore sperantes ut illo remedio ante incolumis servaretur; qui tamen ipse triduo superstes obiit. 58. Romanus porro imperator, uti a nobis in superioribus dictum est, cum erga monachos omnes fidem plurimam haberet cultumque iis adhiberet, præ reliquis tamen eximie Sergium virum monastica institutione præclarum observabat. Erat hic magistri Cosmæ frater 920 Photii patriarchæ nepos, animi ingenuitate quam carnali cognatione nobilior. Ad summam enim virtutem scientiamque evaserat, ut nec utra præstaret discernere facile posset; sic nimirum accurate omnique perfectione utramque excoluerat. Florebat in eo inter alia dis-


[981] {#pg.109.981}
ἤθους καὶ τὸ μέτριον τοῦ φρονήματος. Οὐ γὰρ ἀνέσπα πᾶς ὀφρῦς ὡς οἱ νῦν σοφοί, οὐδὲ ἀλαζών τις ἐδόκει καὶ ὑπερήφανος, ἀλλὰ τὸν λόγον μὲν εἶχε γλυκύτερον μέλιτος ἀποστάζοντα, τὸ ἦθος δὲ πάγιον καὶ σταθερόν, [P. 592] ταπεινὸν δὲ τὸ φρόνημα. Τοῦτον δὴ τὸν ἀοίδιμον ἀδιαλείπτως μεθ᾿ ἑαυτοῦ εἶχεν ὁ βασιλεύς, κανόνα ὄντως καὶ στάθμην τὸν αὐτοῦ βίον ἀεὶ ῥυθμίζοντα. Ὃς πολλὰ παρήνει τῷ βασιλεῖ τῶν παίδων ἐπιμελεῖσθαι καὶ μὴ ἀπαιδεύτους ἐᾶν εἰς πονηρίαν ἐκκλίνοντας, μή ποτε αὐτοὺς πάθοι τὸ Ἡλί, τιμωρίαν ἐκ τῆς τῶν παίδων παρανομίας ἐκτίσας.
νθ΄. Ἐπειδὴ δὲ τοῦ παλατίου τοῦτον οἱ παῖδες κατήγαγον καὶ ἐν τῇ Πρώτῃ νήσῳ ἐξώρισαν, πάλιν τοῦτον εἶχε τῶν συμφορῶν παραμύθιον καὶ τῶν θλίψεων ἀκεσώδυνον φάρμακον. Ὧι συνῆν τότε καὶ Πόλυευκτος μοναχὸς εὐλαβέστατος, καὶ αὐτὸς τὰ εἰκότα παραμυθούμενος· ὃς τοῦ πατριάρχου Θεοφυλάκτου τελευτήσαντος ἐπὶ τοῦ βασιλέως Κωνσταντίνου προχειρίζεται Κωνσταντινουπόλεως.
ξ΄. Ἐπεὶ δὲ πολλοῖς τρόποις βούλεται ὁ Θεὸς σώζειν τὸν ἄνθρωπον, συνεχώρησε καὶ τὸν βασιλέα Ῥωμανὸν ἀδοκήτῳ περιπεσεῖν συμφορᾷ, ἵνα δι᾿ αὐτῆς σωφρονισθεὶς καὶ τῶν οἰκείων ἐν συναισθήσει παραπτωμάτων γενόμενος σωτηρίας ἀξιωθῇ. Συνεχώρησε γὰρ ἐπαναστῆναι αὐτῷ τὸν υἱὸν αὐτοῦ Στέφανον, ὥσπερ ποτὲ Ἀβεσσαλὼν ἐπανέστη τῷ οἰκείῳ πατρὶ τῷ Δαβίδ. Συμβούλοις οὖν οὗτος πρὸς τοῦτο χρησάμενος τῷ ἀπὸ μοναχῶν Μαριανῷ καὶ Βασιλείῳ τῷ πρωτοσπαθαρίῳ τῷ Πετεινάκῃ καὶ Μανουὴλ τῷ Κουρτίκῃ, συνειδότων αὐτῷ καὶ τῶν ἄλλων βασιλέων, τοῦτον τοῦ παλατίου κακῶς κατήγαγε καὶ ἐν τῇ Πρώτῃ νήσῳ ἐξορίσας ἔκειρε μοναχόν.
α΄. Ὑπελείφθη οὖν αὐτοκράτωρ Κωνσταντῖνος ὁ τούτου γαμβρός· ὃς παραυτίκα Βάρδαν τὸν τοῦ Φωκᾶ τῇ τοῦ μαγίστρου ἀξίᾳ τιμήσας, χρόνῳ πολλῷ τὴν ἐν πολέμοις ἀνδραγαθίαν ἐπιδειξάμενος, δομέστικον τῶν σχολῶν προχειρίζεται, εἶτα Βασίλειον, ὃν ἐπίκλην Πετεινάκης, πατρίκιον καὶ μέγαν ἑταιρειάρχην. Τῷ δὲ τοῦ γένους τῶν Ἀργυρῶν Μαριανῷ-τὰ μοναχικὰ ἀποδύσας πατρίκιον καὶ κόμητα τοῦ στάθλου πεποίηκεν, ὡσαύτως καὶ Μανουὴλ τὸν λεγόμενον Κουρτίκην πατρίκιον καὶ δρουγγάριον τῆς βίγλης· οὕστινας τρεῖς οὐ μετὰ πολὺν χρόνον ἡ τοῦ Θεοῦ δικαία κρίσις, [P. 593] ὡς εἰς Χριστὸν Κύριον παροινήσαντας καὶ χεῖρας ἀδίκως ἐπιβαλόντας, τῆς βασιλείας ὀρεγομένους, μετῆλθεν· ἐπὶ καθοσιώσει γὰρ καταληφθέντες ἑκάτερος αὐτῶν οἰκτίστῳ θανάτῳ τὸ ζῆν ἀπέρῥηξαν. Τὰ δὲ περὶ αὐτῶν πλατύτερα τε καὶ ἐπεξεργαστικώτερα ἐν τῇ προηγουμένῃ ἐξηγήσει ἐκθήσομαι.
β΄. Μετὰ δὲ ἡμέρας, τῇ κζ΄ τοῦ Ἰανουαρίου μηνός, ὑποπτεύσας Κωνσταντῖνος τὸν τότε βασιλέα Στέφανον καὶ Κωνσταντῖνον τὸν ἀδελφὸν αὐτοῦ μή ποτε κατ᾿ αὐτοῦ τὰ ὅμοια πράξωνται, καὶ λογισάμενος, ὅπερ ἦν εἰκὸς, ὅτι εἰ τοῦ ἰδίου πατρὸς οὐκ ἐφεί-


[982] {#pg.109.982}
cretionis donum morumque gratia, animi sensus modestia. Neque enim supercilia attrahebat, uti nostrates sapientes in more positum habent, neque vero arrogantem ac superbum præferebat, sed sermo illi melle dulcior ex ore stillabat. Firma indoles ac constans; sensus animi modestus ac humilis. Hunc itaque virum inclytum imperator una secum continuo habebat, ad cujus normam atque regulam vitæ suæ rationes componeret. Monebat ille serioque hortabatur ut liberorum curam ageret, nec indisciplinatos ad nequitiam declinare sineret, ne idem atque Heli illi accideret, ac liberorum piacula ipse lueret; quod et ita accidit.
59. Quod vero aula dejectum liberi in Protem insulam relegaverant, hunc rursus calamitatem solatium ac ærumnarum angustiarumque lene levamen habebat. Aderat etiam pariter Polyuectus reverendissimus monachus, qui ipse perinde ac quem dicebam, Sergius, adversi casus molestiam convenienter apteque leniebat. Hic postea Theophylacto humanis exempto a Constantino imperatore patriarcha provectus est.
921 60. Quandoquidem autem Deus multis modis salutem homini præstare constituit, Romanum imperatorem citra omnem exspectationem in calamitatem incidere permisit, ut is illa castigatus ac de superioris vitæ delictis admonitus salutem consequeretur. Permisit namque Stephanum filium in eum insurgere, uti olim Absalom in Davidem patrem suum. Mariani itaque exmonachi Argiri, Basilique protospatharii Petinacis, necnon Manuelis Curticis consiliis abusus, consciis etiam reliquis imperatoribus, patrem aula perperam deturbavit, inque Próte insula relegatum monachi ritu detondit.
1. Constantinus itaque Romani gener imperator relictus est. Is confestim Bardam Phocæ filium magistri dignitate auctum, quippe longi temporis experientia bellicis facinoribus clara sæpius laude functum atque probatum, legionum domesticum præfecit. Tum Basilium, cui cognomen Petinades, patricium creat ac magnum hetæriarcham. Marianum Argyrorum stirpe satum, detracto monachi habitu patricii decoratum infulis, comitem stabuli facit. Similiter quoque Manuelem dictum Curticem patricium et excubiarum drungarium; quos tres viros, brevi interjecto tempore, justa Dei ultio insecuta est, quod in Christum domini debacchati essent ac inique in eum manus injecissent, cum scilicet imperandi libido eos incessisset. Majestatis enim rei 922 peracti miserando prorsus exitu horum quilibet vitam finere. Cæterum quæ ad illos spectant fusius operosiusque exsequar, ubi ex professo rem narraturus.
2. Post dies quadraginta, Januarii mensis die septimo supra vigesimum, suspectos habens Constantinus Stephanum imperatorem ejusque fratrem Constantinum, ne quando in se quoque paria attentarent; ac secum reputans, quod et par erat,


[983] {#pg.109.983}
cos qui nec patri pepercissent haud sibi parcituros,  convivio acceptos jamque mensæ accumbentes, eum adhuc in ore cibus esset, per Tornicios, quos vocant, et Marianum patricium ac reliquos in rem paratos arripit, aulaque dejectos in vicinis urbi insulis clericorum ritu detondit.
3. Haud ita post, invisendi patris impetrata venia, in insulam Protem venere; quo conspecto monachi habitu, intolerabili luctu correpti sunt. Quibus pater collacrymatus ait: Filios genui et exaltavi, ipsi vero spreverunt me. Sic deinde relegati sunt, Stephanus quidem in Præconesum; inde Mitylenem. Constantinum vero Tenedum, inde Samothraciam; ubi et rebellionem 923 molitus a custodibus necatus est. Michaëlem Christophori imperatoris filium detractis imperialibus calceis clericum fecit.
4. Imperatore Romano in insula exsule, Theophylactus patriarcha et Theophanes patricius ac aecubitor consilium inierunt, ut Romanum in aulam reducerent. Eoque cum ipso communicato consilio ac sententiam pertracto, opportunum tempus captabant quo molitioni finem imponerent. Deprehensa autem conjuratione, delatisque Constantino imperatori, qui ejus auctores ac consilii forent, in eos animadvertit. Ac Theophanem quidem patricium exsilio relegavit; Georgium vero protospatharium ac pincernam, nec non Thomam  primicerium verberibus subactos detonsosque ac per mediam urbem pompa traductos, ita in exsilium misit.
5. Mense Decembri, indictione sexta, quidam insidias moliti sunt adversus Constantinum imperatorem, hoc consilio ut Stephanum imperatorem ex insula, ubi agebat, in palatium reducerent. Delata Constantino conjuratione per Michaëlem, quem Diabolinum vocant, captis imperator qui conjurati essent, aliis nares auresque præscidit, alios intolerando verberum cruciatus exanimatos, asinisque impositos ac per mediam urbem ignominiose traductos ac exsilio relegavit.
924 9. Quinta decima Julii mensis, indictione sexta, moritur Romanus imperator, in insula quam  Protem vocant. Ejus cadaver in urbem relatum, in illius depositum monasterio est.
Huc usque Francisci Combefisii editio Scriptorum post Theophanem.

santo, πῶς αὐτοῦ φείσονται, ἑστιάσας αὐτούς, ἤδη ἐν τῇ τραπέζῃ καθεζομένους, ἔτι τῆς βρώσεως οὔσης ἐν τῷ στόματι αὐτῶν, ἀνήρπασαν τούτους οἵ τε λεγόμενοι Τορνίκιοι καὶ ὁ πατρίκιος Μαριανὸς καὶ οἱ λοιποὶ οἱ πρὸς τοῦτο ἡτοιμασμένοι, καὶ τοῦ παλατίου καταγαγόντες καὶ ἐν ταῖς πλησιαζούσαις νήσοις αὐτοὺς περιορίσαντες κληρικοὺς ἀπέκειραν.
γ΄. Μετ᾿ ὀλίγον οὖν αἰτησάμενοι τὸν ἴδιον πατέρα θεάσασθαι ἐν τῇ Πρώτῃ νήσῳ παρεγένοντο, καὶ τοῦτον ἐν τῷ μοναχικῷ σχήματι θεασάμενοι πένθει κατεσχέθησαν ἀφορήτῳ. Οἷς ἐπιδακρύσας ὁ πατὴρ ἔφη· Υἱοὺς ἐγέννησα καὶ ὕψωσα, αὐτοὶ δέ με ἠθέτησαν. Εἶθ᾿ οὕτως ἐξωρίσθησαν, ὁ μὲν Στέφανος εἰς Προικόνησον, ἀπὸ δὲ Προικονήσου εἰς Ῥόδον, ἀπὸ δὲ Ῥόδου εἰς Μιτυλήνην, ὁ δὲ Κωνσταντῖνος εἰς Τένεδον, ἐκεῖθεν εἰς Σαμοθράκην, ἐν ᾗ καὶ ἀνταρσίαν μελετήσας παρὰ τῶν αὐτὴν φυλασσόντων ἐσφάγη. Μιχαὴλ δὲ τὸν τοῦ Χριστοφόρου υἱὸν τὰ βασιλικὰ πέδιλα ἀφελόμενος κληρικὸν πεποίηκεν.

Ὄντος δὲ τοῦ βασιλέως Ῥωμανοῦ ἐν τῇ νήσῳ, ὁ πατριάρχης Θεοφύλακτος καὶ Θεοφάνης ὁ πατρίκιος καὶ παρακοιμώμενος βουλὴν ἐβουλεύσαντο ὥστε πάλιν ἐν τῷ παλατίῳ τοῦτον ἀγαγεῖν· ἣν καὶ αὐτῷ ἀνακοινωσάμενοι καὶ παραπείσαντες τοῦτον, ἐκαιροσκόπουν πότε πέρας τῇ τοιαύτῃ βουλῇ ἐπιθῶσιν. Ἐπεὶ δὲ τὰ τῆς βουλῆς ἐφωράθη καὶ κατεμηνύθη Κωνσταντίνῳ βασιλεῖ, τοὺς συνεργοὺς ταύτης ἠμύνετο· τὸν μὲν γὰρ πατρίκιον Θεοφάνην ἐξώρισεν, τὸν δὲ πρωτοσπαθάριον καὶ πιγκέρνην Γεώργιον καὶ Θωμᾶν πριμικήριον δείρας καὶ κουρεύσας καὶ μέσον τῆς πόλεως θριαμβεύσας οὕτως ὑπερορίᾳ παρέπεμψε.

[P. 594] Ταύτης οὖν μηνυθείσης τῆς ἐπιβουλῆς Κωνσταντίνῳ διὰ Μιχαὴλ τοῦ Διαβολίνου λεγομένου, τοὺς ἐπιβούλους κρατήσας· ὁ βασιλεὺς τῶν μὲν τὰς ῥῖνας καὶ τὰ ὦτα ἀπέτεμεν, τοὺς δὲ δαρμῷ ἀφορήτῳ ὑπέβαλε, καὶ ὄνοις ἐπικαθίσας διὰ μέσης τῆς πόλεως ἐθριάμβευσε καὶ ἐξορίᾳ παρέπεμψε.
ς΄. Πεντεκαιδεκάτῃ Ἰουλίῳ μηνί, ἰνδικτιῶνος ς΄, Ῥωμανὸς ὁ βασιλεὺς ἐν τῇ Πρώτῃ νήσῳ τελευτᾷ· καὶ τὸ σῶμα αὐτοῦ ἐν τῇ πόλει διακομισθὲν ἐν τῇ αὐτοῦ ἀπετέθη μονῇ.
Armenians; for the Assyrians trace their name from the Assyrians, but the Armenians from a certain Armenius so called, who came from the city of Armenia in Thessaly, and who accompanied Jason on his expedition.
2. This Leo, having been crowned with the imperial diadem by the Patriarch Nicephorus and inaugurated by him, professed in writing that he was of the orthodox faith. Yet the wolf, covered in sheep’s clothing, did not escape notice as a good shepherd. For while he was binding the diadem upon his wicked head, it seemed to him as though he were suffering the prick of pain from thorns and briars. And when the customary rite was completed, the malicious man departed for the palace, while the people shouted acclamations. But when he reached [the area] before the Bronze Gates, at the circular place paved with porphyry marble, he offered thanks to the image of Christ our God situated there, whether in truth or merely in pretense. There he doffed the robe he had been wearing, which shone with rose-colored hues, and the *colobium*—which they call such according to the ancient custom reserved for generals—and gave them to Michael, who was his equerry, a man from Amorium. As he put this on, it occurred to those in the know as an omen of the lordship of the empire that would follow Leo. Then, having come to the place called Scyla, he trod upon Leo's robe with a forceful step. Leo, having understood the matter, conjectured that some rebellion was being stirred up against him that night.
3. He therefore turned his mind to the monk living in Philomelium, who, in the time of Bardanes, had told him what would come to pass. He sends him gifts and offerings appropriate for the fulfillment of his vow. But as that monk had meanwhile departed this life, it happened that another monk named Sabbatius—who had also heard the words of the former monk—had succeeded into his cell and place, and he was opposed to the veneration of the holy icons. When he saw what had been sent by the Emperor, he made a show of sending them away, because he [Leo] offered veneration to the holy icons; for which reason, he, as a wicked seer, predicted that the Lord would overthrow his empire, and sent a letter to that effect. Deceived by these things, the Emperor summoned Theodotus Cassiteras, who, even before Leo had seized the empire, having heard a certain woman possessed of a Pythonic spirit walking in the marketplace and crying out that the Strategos of the Anatolics was about to receive the empire of Michael, had taken Leo with him and gone to the Church of Saint Paul in the Orphanotropheion. There they made a covenant of mutual keeping and trust one toward the other; and he [Theodotus] insisted to him, "It has been revealed to me by divine vision concerning you, that you shall quickly attain the empire." Having summoned this Theodotus, D Leo showed him what had been sent. He, grasping this opportunity, piled evil upon evil, and made mention of another monk living in the house of Maurianus, asserting that unheard-of things were being performed by him, and praising and proclaiming him as another great Anthony. He persuaded Leo that this was the state of affairs, and then, coming to the monk, he spoke these things with a wicked mind: "The following night the Emperor will come to you in a humble state, [with the intention of] falling before you, and he is to pass [into the church] quickly
under my guidance, and he shall be prepared [to do so]; but if you listen to me, you will heap curses upon him, and declare that he will die shortly, unless he follows the doctrine and faith of Leo the Isaurian. If he does this, he will see more than seventy years of imperial rule, and will see his children's children sitting upon the throne, and he will be exalted with the honor of the thirteenth apostle. But if he acts otherwise, he will be subject to countless evils, and will shortly lose both his empire and his life."
Now, therefore, in the darkness of the night, the Emperor, with Cassiteras as his companion, went to the monk and was immediately rebuked by him quite harshly, as if he had foreseen the former's disguise and deception. "It is not fitting," he said, "for one who is emperor to feign the role of a private citizen." Struck with stupefaction by these words, the Emperor allowed himself to be easily persuaded to the opinion of abolishing the holy images; and immediately, vomiting out the venom of impiety that he had stored up in his mind, and agitated by furies, he led the beginning of the heresy.
4. Seeking to join to himself like-minded men, he finds John, called the Grammarian—rather another Jannes or Simon—who was also called Hylzilas, which in the Hebrew tongue is interpreted as "precursor and helper of the devil." He was the son of Pancratius the *sciastes* (we might call him a hat-maker), whom a demon had possessed since he was a boy, and whose fame for divination by means of a basin and for acts of filth and lust had spread far abroad. He also found Constantine, a priest, the son of a cobbler, who had taught law in the time of Phoracius, but having fled on account of accusations, he became a monk and took the name Antonius; he was a man devoted to fables and mockery, living a base life. Having become the superior of the monastery called "of the Metropolitans," he taught these same things to the monks he governed; and later, by the permission of God, he even became Bishop of Syllaeum. He found also others of the same mind as himself.
Meanwhile, a comet appeared in the shape of two bright, moon-like orbs, which would come together and then separate into diverse shapes, even forming the shape of a man without a head, just such as had appeared in the time of Michael the Curopalates, Emperor. Some, interpreting this, said that the headless [figure] was a head, foreshadowing Thomas, who would later take up tyranny and with his fellow conspirators assault the city. Indeed, terrible earthquakes, plagues, droughts, and fever-heat also occurred during the days of this man who was hateful to God.
5. In the same year, he summoned the celebrated Patriarch Nicephorus along with the bishops who were with him before the senate, and said: "It certainly does not escape your notice that some have arisen who say that there is no need to venerate the images." To whom the holy Nicephorus first replied: "They are to be honored, O Emperor, the holy images of the saints, as they existed from the beginning by divine nod. For the holy cloth which Christ once took in His hands and held to His own face, and was marked with His own image, not by human hand, is deservedly held in honor and is worthy of veneration. Then, too, the images of the holy apostles which they themselves painted with their own hands (most notably of Christ Himself and of her who gave birth to Him), and which even now in Rome...
image erected in the churches throughout the land; then, those who were discovered to possess any image of Christ or of the saints in their own homes, he condemned to dire torments and to death, and he slaughtered many men of renown and distinction, as well as an infinite multitude of monks.
7. But since Leo had adopted the daughter of the Michael from Amorion—whom he had also taken from the spiritual font—and had honored him with the dignity of patrician and commander of the Excubitors, and as this man, with a bold and insolent tongue, was reproaching Leo as a destroyer of the holy images and asserting that his wife ought to be united in incestuous wedlock, the emperor, thinking he was plotting tyranny, ordered him to be bound and tied to a stake at the palace baths, which they call the Pithecium, and cast into the furnace fire to be consumed; and as the man was on the point of destruction, the emperor stood by as a spectator. However, the Augusta Theodosia, with tearful supplications, rescued the man from the executioner, and the emperor ordered the accused to be released for the time being. But these were the words he said to his own wife: "O woman, today you have delivered my soul from the judgment that was to come, yet perhaps you will soon deprive me of life; but you and our children shall see what is to come to pass." For the time of his destruction had long been revealed to him from a certain Sibylline book of oracles. For it contained a representation of this kind: it depicted the entire figure of a lion. On the head was inscribed the letter X, and on the back the letter Φ, and in the middle of the beast, piercing its very flanks, a bloody sword or blade was fixed; which, having shown to Stephen, known by the surname Capitoliges, he said to him: "What does this signify?" But he said: "Let it be, my lord; do not let me even behold the drawing." But he would not accept this. Then Stephen, considering it within himself, says to the emperor: "This lion stands for the sign of the empire, and between the letter X and the Φ, some evil threatens him." And the emperor: "What do the Φ and the X mean?" He says: "Between the birth of Christ and the Lights" (that is, the Epiphany). But he was also troubled by that which had been said to him through the patrician Bardanes by a monk at Philomelium, and by the matter of the official robe he had put on, the hem of which he had trodden upon while entering the palace to take up the imperial power. In addition to these things, the vision seen by his mother also filled him with terror. At the time it happened, she had considered it of no value, but now it pressed upon and tormented her soul even more grievously. For as she was frequently resorting to the divine church in Blachernae and constantly attending it with pious prayers, a maiden appeared to her, surrounded by a large retinue of candidati, while the entire church was overflowing with blood. Then the maiden commanded one of those standing by to fill a pot with blood and present it to the mother of Leo to drink. When she excused herself, citing her long years, during which she had tasted neither flesh nor anything containing coagulated blood, and that therefore she had no need of this pot, the maiden replied with anger: "And how is it, then, that your son does not cease from filling me with much blood, and from provoking this my son and God?" From that time on, she entreated her son many times to depart from the heresy of the iconoclasts, [P. 407] [index: entries preserved]
those who delivered sacred images to the flames and abolished them, mourning this vision lugubriously. But another nocturnal vision also cast no less terror than the first. For he heard a man named Michael calling out to the patriarch Tarasius of glorious memory—who had long since departed among the dead—and heard him strike Leo with a lethal blow and thrust him over a precipice into the vast abyss lying beneath.
8. In the days of the impious Leo, the Bulgarians came with no one to meet them or block their approach, arriving even at the city gate. Crumus, according to the custom of his people, performed a sacrifice outside the Golden Gate and slaughtered many men and beasts. Having wetted his feet at the seashore, bathed, and purified his army, he was received with auspicious shouts and favorable signs by his own men; he proceeded through the middle of his concubines, who prostrated themselves before him in reverence and praised him, all this while everyone watched from the wall, and no one dared to hinder him or shoot a dart at him. When he had accomplished everything he desired and whatever he willed, he encircled the city and threw up a rampart against it. The siege continued for several days, and after all the fields outside the city had been plundered, he began to demand a great quantity of gold and clothing, and a certain number of choice maidens as well. Wherefore Leo, having deliberated with his nobles, signified to Crumus: "Come with a few unarmed men to the seashore, and we ourselves will come up to the shore unarmed in a boat, and we will hold discussions; whatever you have asked for, we will do." Having held these discussions, through the night they led out three armed men and concealed them in certain chambers of the Hall outside the Blachernae gate, giving them a sign: "When we are speaking with Crumus, we will perform a certain signal; when you see this, come out from your ambush and kill him." The next day, the Bulgarians having stationed themselves at the Church of the Holy Anargyroi outside the city gate, Crumus descended to the sea as agreed, along with three others: his logothete, and Constantine, called "the Patziki," who many years before had fled to Bulgaria and whom he had taken as a son from the sister of Crumus. These four, having separated themselves from their people unarmed, descended to the sea as agreed, not knowing the trap set for them. Soon, those from the city also went out in a boat, wishing to parley with Crumus, and having received assurances, they dismounted from the ship. Crumus jumped down from his horse and sat upon the ground, and the son of Constantine held his horse, which was saddled and bridled. While they were conversing, one of those from the city, who was Exabules, gave the signal by uncovering his head with his hand. Crumus noticed this, and offended by the evidence of the deed, he recoiled, and having already readied his horse, he was mounted by his companions and sat upon it. Then the people on the wall cried out: "The Cross has won!" And Crumus took to flight. Those who had been lying in wait in the houses...
returned to their tents, not at all concerned for their own safety. But during the night, the enemy troops fell upon them and brought all of them to that sleep of iron, while only their commander escaped by boarding a merchant vessel and sailing away. Upon learning this, the leader of the Agarenes placed his men on two transport ships and pursued him, and having caught him at the island of Cos, he impaled him there. Thereafter, Oryphas (for that was the man’s name), by order of the emperor, assembled a naval force, distributed forty gold pieces to each, and embarked them on ships. They set out for Crete, but having failed to achieve anything, they returned; many of them were lost there, and those who were left behind were called the "Quadragenarii." On this island, Cyril, the miracle-working bishop of Gortyna of that island, was slaughtered by the Agarenes as a spotless sacrifice for Christ; his precious blood, bubbling forth even to this day, performs miracles.
So much for him. The impious Michael, having fallen severely ill with a urinary blockage and being tormented by pains in his kidneys, ended his life miserably. His wretched funeral procession was carried out, and he was laid to rest in the Church of the Holy Apostles; his son Theophilus, along with his mother Euphrosyne, took possession of the empire.
Roman Emperor Theophilus, son of Michael the Stammerer, Iconoclast, 12 years
1. In the year of the world 6323, of the divine incarnation 825, the Roman emperor Theophilus, son of Michael the Stammerer, Iconoclast, reigned for 12 years. His mother Euphrosyne, wishing to provide him with a wife, brought together several maidens of incomparable beauty. Among them there was one of great beauty named Icasia, and another named Theodora. The mother, having given him a golden apple, told him to give it to the one whose form pleased him most. Theophilus, stunned by the beauty of Icasia, said, "From woman have evils flowed." To which she replied with composure: "But also, better things from woman have flourished." Astonished and offended by her response, he dismissed her and gave the golden apple to Theodora, who was from the land of the Paphlagonians. Both were crowned by the patriarch Anthony in the Oratory of Saint Stephen on the day of Pentecost. From there, they proceeded to the Great Church, where he bestowed generous gifts upon the patriarch, the clergy, and the senate. Icasia, frustrated in her hope for the empire, built a monastery where, having cut her hair, she lived a life governed by virtue and pleasing to God until the end of her days, leaving behind many ornaments of her own genius. Euphrosyne, however, the emperor's mother, voluntarily departing from the palace, lived a private life in the monastery of Gastria.
2. A horse-race having occurred, the great candelabrum was brought out, which in the slaughter of the Armenian Leo had been cut by a sword.
not kept for Alexius the written pledge given through me?” The emperor, moved to uncontrollable wrath and fury, dragged him violently from the altar and, having inflicted upon him no light lashes, sent him into exile—not only because he had been reproved by him, but also because he learned that he worshipped and honored the holy icons and denounced his own impiety. Not long after, however, when the patriarch had reproached him at the Great Church, he brought the archbishop back with entreaties. And when he judged himself unworthy of the priesthood because of the injury he had suffered, the emperor made him steward of the Great Church. He also released Alexius from prison, restored all his property, and held him in honor. He, having cut his hair, built the monastery of Anthemius, finished his life there, and was buried in holiness.
10. In the fifth year of his reign, Manuel, the most famous general of all the East, was held in great esteem by the emperor. Once, when he had some conversation with Myron, the logothete of the drome and father-in-law of Petronas, he was denounced to the emperor as one who aspired to the empire and was plotting terrible things against him. Leo, the protovestiarius, being concerned for the safety of Manuel and defending him, earnestly asserted that these things were falsely alleged against him. When Manuel learned this, and seeking to escape the wrath of the emperor and the accusations, he left the city secretly and went as far as Pylae. Getting into public carriages, he fled as far as the mountain passes of Syria, cutting the hamstrings of his horses to delay pursuit. He declared to the Agarenes, “I am fleeing the wrath of the emperor, and if you do not compel me to abandon my faith, I flee to you. If you receive me on these conditions, send me a pledge of safety.” They, cheered and gladdened by this news, and trusting in it, received him with honor not inferior to that of the Roman emperor. When Theophilus heard this, he fell into great grief and distress of mind. Having consulted with John the Syncellus, he said, “If you truly desire, O emperor, for Manuel to return to you, I am ready to bring this about.” Having taken money and having set off on the journey to the Amermumnes C —purporting to visit those who were held in prison and in bonds—and possessing a written document under the hand of your majesty, through which I will persuade Manuel to return, I expect to accomplish this both by the written document and by my own persuasion, and by his piety, and by the probability that he loves his homeland. The emperor therefore sent him forth, provided with a vast amount of money and gifts. Entering the city, John distributed gifts to those in bonds and, having taken the protosymbulos aside, he managed to speak with Manuel in secret and to give him the written document and the protective cross of the emperor. Having done these things, he returned and reported everything to the emperor. Manuel, having requested of the Amermumnes that he might allow the Roman captives who were held in prison to return, after receiving assurance from him regarding those who were under the
Theophobus held the city which he had occupied by the right of a tyrant. When the emperor learned of this, he was affected by great grief. Fearing, however, that they might join themselves to the Arabs, he came as far as Paphlagonia; and having given his word that they would suffer no harm, he returned to the city, having taken Theophobus with him. The remaining Persians returned to the dwellings previously assigned to each. Furthermore, Theophobus was held in no less affection by the citizens than by the Persians, for the reason that he was orthodox.
17. In the tenth year of the emperor Theophilus, a son was born to him by Theodora, whom he named Michael. As the emperor was proceeding to the Blachernae according to custom, someone met him saying: "The horse upon which your Majesty is riding is mine." The emperor inquired of the count of the stable whose horse it might be. He said: "The count of the Opsikion sent it to your Majesty." On the next day, therefore, having brought the count of the Opsikion (for it happened that he was in the city at that time), he said: "Whose horse is this?" He replied that he had bought it. But when the claimant objected and said that it had been taken from him by force and without any price being paid, the emperor said: "Why did you not pay the price beforehand?" He answered: "I offered him one hundred coins; he was seeking to be enrolled among the Scholarioi, but because he was an ignorant man, he did not obtain this and was unwilling to accept the one hundred coins." The emperor, thus being made certain that the horse had been taken by force, returned it, ordering that he also be enrolled among the Scholarioi. He was not satisfied to receive the horse, but departed having been gifted with two pounds of gold.
18. When the Saracens came against Amorium, the emperor quickly led his forces as far as Cappadocia. But the Amermumnes, choosing out fifty thousand men and placing over them Gundees, a man most famous among the Agarenes, sent them against the emperor. A battle being joined, the emperor was defeated and routed, and he himself escaped from the slaughter with shame, having barely saved himself. The Saracens, having surrounded Amorium on all sides and having attempted it with various battles, since they saw those inside defending the city strenuously, were meditating on breaking off the siege. But a certain disciple of Leo the Philosopher, who was then serving in the garrison, sent word to these men, saying: "If you persevere for only two days, you will overthrow the city." Which is what happened; for it was betrayed by the man named Buditzes and by Manicophanes. There were captured the most distinguished men of the faithful: Theophilus the patrician and general, and Melissenus and Aetius, and Theodorus the protospatharios and the eunuch Craterus, Callistus the tourmarches, Constantine the droungarios, and Basoes the cursor, as well as certain commanders of the tagmata. Wishing to ransom them, the emperor sent ambassadors to the Amermumnes with two hundred centenaria, but he could not overcome his resolve; for he said that even if he were to pay a thousand centenaria of gold in that name to receive the captives, not even one of them would be set free.
19. When the captives were then led away into Syria, many things were suffered at the hands of the protosymboulos, [P. 426]
is the hospital, which they now call Theophilus’s. It was originally the house of the patrician Isidore, who came from Rome with Olybrius under Constantine the Great. For many years thereafter, it was annexed to the imperial treasury and served as a dwelling for noblewomen of ill repute. During the reign of the Emperor Leo the Isaurian, these premises, which had been a brothel, were converted into a hospital. Later, under Constantine, son of Irene, after he had been blinded by his mother, it served as a house. Upon his death, his wife embraced the monastic life and constructed a monastery there, which she called "Metanoia" (that is, repentance). When a huge beam of the dining hall cracked and threatened to collapse, the nuns implored Theophilus, who was passing by, to provide help. He stretched out his head, looked at the house, and was pleased with it; having transferred the nuns to another monastery and decorated the house with every kind of ornament, he dedicated it for the reception of foreigners in the manner of a hospital, enriched it with funds and suburban estates, and named it Theophilus’.
27. The emperor, suffering from dysentery and tortured by severe pains, took counsel with his companions regarding Theophobos the Persian. For knowing that he possessed great trust and love among the Persians—and indeed among not a few of the nobles—he feared that, should he die while Theophobos was still alive, they might set him up as emperor, casting aside his own son, Michael. He therefore sent for him, brought him into the palace, and held him under guard. Later, as his illness grew worse, he ordered him to be beheaded. Seeing the severed head, he said, "Now, Theophobos, you have ceased to live, and I am free of cares." Soon after, he expired with terrible and painful agony. His funeral was conducted at the Church of the Holy Apostles, while that of Theophobos was held in his own house near the residence of Narses. C Meanwhile, as the Persians were inquiring about Theophobos and what indeed had become of him, the courtiers assured them that he was living in the palace. This rumor has prevailed among the Persians to this day, that Theophobos never tasted death.
The Roman Emperors Michael and Theodora
1. In the year of the world 6335, and of the divine incarnation 835, Michael and Theodora reigned as Emperors of the Romans for 14 years; Michael alone for 10 years, and with Basil for 1 year and 4 months; in total 27 years and 4 months. Theodora, being pious, faithful, and orthodox, while her husband was still alive, secretly honored and venerated the holy icons. Acting upon her own resolve, and with the advice and encouragement of Theoctistus, the keeper of the inkstand and logothete, she cast from the Church the impious Patriarch John—even though he was her child's godfather—and relegated him to exile in a monastery at the Stenon called Cleidion. There, because he had gouged out the eyes of the icons of Christ our God, the Theotokos, and the princes of the heavenly host, Theodora had intended to have his own eyes gouged out. However, those whose advice was sought in the matter did not permit it.
bridle of the horse he held, and with the other he took hold of its ear, and brought it to the tameness of a sheep. This pleased the emperor, and he handed him over to Andrew the hetairiarch, to be enrolled among the companions and to serve the royal horses.
11. Now this Basil was from Macedonia, from the villages of Adrianople. He attached himself to the strategos of Macedonia, Tzantzes, to serve him; he was twenty-five years old. Having gained no benefit from him, he came to the city. And having entered within the Golden Gate, because he was exhausted from the journey and it was Sunday, toward evening he lay down in the porch of Saint Diomedes, which had previously served as a temple to the Sun. For it was a public church, and was under the charge of a warden named Nicholas; to whom in the night appeared one who said: "Arise, lead the emperor inside." He, having arisen, found no one except Basil lying there like a poor man, and having turned back, he went to sleep again in his bed. In the same way this happened a second time. But at the third time, with greater force and insistence, he struck his side with a sword, saying: "Do you not see him lying outside the gate? He himself is the emperor; lead him inside." He, therefore, went out with haste, trembling, and finding Basil with a bag and a staff, he brought him inside. And the next morning, he bathed him and changed his garments, and having made him his sworn brother, he rejoiced with him. There was a brother of Nicholas, a physician serving the aforementioned Theophilitzes, who also by chance came to his brother Nicholas, saw Basil, and, having marveled at both the stature of his age and his appearance, asked his brother whence he was. He told him everything except the vision. A few days later, therefore, when Theophilitzes was looking for a brave man for his horses, the physician was present and related what he knew about Basil. Basil was therefore brought quickly, and upon seeing him, Theophilitzes conceived an affection for him. Since he was a sturdy youth and large-headed, he called him "Cephalas," and gave him to serve his horses. Hence, as has been said, he is given to the emperor Michael. And the emperor Michael, having brought Basil before his mother, said: "Come, see, mother, the man whom I have obtained now." She, having gone out and seen him, turned away, saying: "This is, my son, he who is to destroy our lineage." But the emperor was in no way persuaded, nor did he listen to his mother.
12. In the eleventh year of his reign, Methodius, the patriarch in holiness, died, and in his place was ordained Ignatius, the son of the Curopalates who had once been emperor. And when the Bulgarians were plundering Thrace and Macedonia, Theodora sent an army and checked their insolence and restrained their attack.
consumed it along with her, and from there he went back on foot to the palace.
18. And what is far worse is this: he maintained a certain sodality, these were some sort of Satyrs, dissolute in every kind of turpitude and lust, whom he held in honor while he showed utter contempt for divine things. He provided them with priestly vestments interwoven with gold and stoles according to the custom of pontiffs, and compelled them to perform holy offices in a shameful and profane manner. He called the leader of these ones patriarch, Gryllus by name, and the remaining eleven, metropolitans of special and illustrious sees; unless he himself was a partner with them, he did not even deem the very citadel of the empire a thing to be desired by him. For the bishop of the first see of Colonia was the twelfth, and the emperor Michael himself was [the thirteenth]. And they fulfilled the canticles by playing upon citharas; and things that are said in secret they imitated, resounding them softly in a shrill way; but the exclamations which are properly uttered with a higher and modulated voice during the sacred rite, they gave forth with free impetus and a louder sound. They had golden vessels adorned with gems, and filling them with vinegar and mustard, they dispensed them to those who showed a disposition to communicate, and in that manner they mocked the undefiled mysteries. This Gryllus, therefore, would ride upon a white mule, performing public processions, and he rejoiced, having such a choir following him. And it happened once that as the blessed Ignatius was going along with an ecclesiastical litany, he encountered them; and pulling back his chasuble along with his fellow-celebrants, [Gryllus] began to play more intensely than the instruments, and overwhelmed the clergy with countless and most shameful language. The saint rebuked the man’s insolence; but he kept nothing of what he had been warned and corrected about. In the meantime, however, he did turn aside from the path. This most demented of men exhibited to himself a shameful laughter and cacophony, by which the holy oblation was afflicted with far-reaching procacious injury and disgrace; the divine oracles were also afflicted, being subjected to their impious and impure pursuits and jests, and pronounced amid those things.
19. Not long after the most holy Ignatius had been ordered to cease and was removed from the throne of the patriarchate, when the holy rite was being performed in a mimic fashion before the emperor’s table in the presence of Photius, and was being mocked with the most shameful imitation by those contemptible and execrable men, he [Photius] not only did not rebuke or argue against and abolish this most absurd custom, but even, as a most wicked man, he confirmed the wicked act, praying for that God-hated Theophilus (who is also called Gryllus), whom the most profligate and dull-witted of men, the emperor, called his own patriarch—a man, forsooth, industrious at mocking even in priestly vestments and divine oracles, and at emitting, like a mule, most loud rumblings, and at calling out to them the one thing worthy of this, that all should drink from it and take meats with mustard and vinegar. And with his own omophorion the profane man profanely sealed the profane princedom for the profane one, raising his voice in laughter amid the fools as a fool himself.
mocking the illustrious Ignatius for not being yielding in matters and, as he himself thought, for not being wafted about like a weathercock with every wind. Moreover, he engaged in a contest with the wretched emperor Michael to see who could drink the most, and he surpassed him by ten cups; for while Michael had filled himself with fifty, [P. 439] this Photius, having drunk sixty, boasted and bragged that he had not been overcome by the wine.
20. And once, when Michael’s mother was living in the palace, with Gryllos practicing his wicked deeds along with him, he feigned that this man was the blessed Ignatius, and summoned her to be blessed by him. So, as she came out with piety and reverence, she threw herself onto the floor, asking for a prayer; for he had been cleverly hiding his beard until that moment. But he, jumping up immediately, emitted a breaking of wind from his belly and, adding unseemly words, said: "We, my lady, bestow what we have." And he filled the whole chamber with the stench. But she, having divined what was to happen, declared that he would be far removed from the providence and hand of God.
21. Further, while Theodora was holding the empire, Bogaris, the ruler of Bulgaria, hearing that a woman held the helm of the Roman Empire, sent messengers to her, declaring that he was breaking the treaties and marching against the Romans. It was answered back to him: "You shall find that I also will march against you, and we hope to overcome you. But if not, even then I shall come out superior to you, having the victory clearly assured, because it is a woman, and certainly not a man, whom you will have been defeated by." Because of this answer, the barbarian became milder, maintained the peace, and renewed the bonds of affection.
22. She then made inquiries regarding the monk Theodore Koupharas, who had been taken captive a long time before. Bogaris also, seizing the occasion, sent word to her about his own sister, who was likewise a captive and held in the emperor’s palace. She had learned letters and the order of the Christians, and the reverence and glory accorded to the Divine, and when she happened to return to her brother, she never ceased praising the Christian cause and exhorting her brother to transfer his mind to the true faith. Although he, suffering from his incredulity, did not yet wish to open his eyes to the truth, when famine began to devastate the whole region, he was forced to cross the Roman borders.
23. In his third year, the emperor Michael promoted Antigonus, the son of Bardas, to be Domestic of the Schools; and to his other son—after giving him a wife, with whom he was suspected of having an illicit relationship—he granted the rank of Monostrategos of the West. Upon his brief death, on the fourth day of Easter week, Michael adorned Bardas, his own uncle, with the insignia of Caesar.

Exasperated by this, he reported it to the emperor in tears, and said that he had not risen for him, intending by this to insult the imperial majesty itself. Upon hearing this, the emperor banished the paracōimōmenos Damianus to Saint Mamas, and appointed his own protostrator, Basil, as paracōimōmenos. When Caesar saw this, he was greatly aggrieved and sought to kill Basil.
40. In the eleventh year of his reign, Emperor Michael compelled Basil to divorce his wife, Maria, and gave him in marriage Eudocia Ingerina, commanding him to have her as his lawful wife; for she had been the emperor’s concubine, and he loved her greatly because she was exceedingly beautiful. Her former husband, Basil, having been given gold and much wealth, was sent back to his own estates. He then betrothed his own sister, Thecla, to Basil, that he might have her privately. However, Caesar and Basil were mutually suspicious of one another, each seeking how he might destroy the other. Basil therefore slandered Caesar to the emperor, alleging that he was plotting against him. But the emperor heard these things as nonsense. Therefore, Basil, hastening to convince the emperor, reconciled himself with Symbatius the patrician and logothete of the dromos, the son-in-law of Caesar; and by oaths they confirmed a concord between them. Basil convinced Symbatius with fearful oaths that the emperor loved him very much and wished to promote Caesar, but could not do so because of his father-in-law. Deceived by these things, Symbatius conspired against Caesar Bardas, his own father-in-law, and entering in to the emperor, he swore an oath and convinced him, saying: "Caesar wishes to kill you," and he even revealed the details of the plot. The emperor, believing the oaths of Symbatius and confirmed by the words of Basil, inwardly harbored designs against Caesar. But Basil, having prepared everything against Caesar, and being unable to do anything in the city, persuaded the emperor to move the fleet against Crete. While this was happening, Leo the philosopher advised D Caesar Bardas to withdraw and to guard himself against Basil. But Caesar replied that the emperor was more inclined toward Basil. Now, as the procession of the Annunciation was being celebrated in the Chalcoprateia, after the Gospel had been read, Patriarch Photius ascended with the emperor, along with Caesar and Basil the paracōimōmenos, to the catechumena; and as the patriarch held in his hands the precious body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, the emperor and Basil, having dipped [their pens], signed the venerated crosses, binding themselves by oath to Caesar to ensure his faith, so that, free from every fear, he might undertake that expedition with them. But Leo the philosopher openly warned Bardas not to go out with them; or if he did, he would not return.

And on the day when the games of the Hippodrome were held, he had them brought forth, stripped, and scourged, and burned their bones at the houses of Maurianus. As for the urn of Copronymus, which was made of green marble by a wonderful craft, he cut it with a saw and made small columns from it in the church he himself had built in the palace of the Pharos.
46. In the month of September, in the fifteenth indiction, Constantine the emperor was born to Michael and Eudocia Ingerina, while Michael was still alive. And when the games of the Hippodrome were held at Saint Mamas, the emperor rode as a Blue, while Constantine the Armenian—the father of Thomas the patrician and drungary of the Watch—was White, Agallianus was Green, and Crassus was Red. The emperor was victorious. And what happened thereafter? For this too is a specimen of the folly of the emperor Michael, in that he thus diminished the strength of the Romans. Leo the Philosopher, who had been bishop of Thessalonica, had advised the emperor Theophilus to construct two clocks, which were to have the same power and run in unison. One he placed at the fortress near Tarsus in Cilicia, and the other was kept in the palace. On them were inscribed for every hour the events that might occur in Syria: for instance, at the first hour, if there were an incursion of Saracens; at the second, if a battle were joined; at the third, if a fire were ignited; at the fourth, if something else occurred; and similarly for the rest. From these twelve inscribed propositions, therefore, if anything happened in Syria, at the hour in which the event took place, a torch was lit from the elevated position there, as the guards were always watching attentively and accurately for the marks set upon them. The torch lit from the fortress called Loulon signaled it to the inhabitants of Mount Argaeus, and again to those at Samos and those at Aegilon, then to those at the mountain of Saint Mamas, from which it reached Cyrizus, then Mocilus, and from there to the mountain of Saint Auxentius, making it manifest in a short time to those standing constant watch at the sun-dial of the Pharos in the palace. Thus, as has been said, while the emperor Michael was riding in the Hippodrome, a torch signaled an incursion of the Saracens. He, so that the spectators of the Hippodrome might not be distracted on the morrow, ordered such torches to be lit no more from the neighboring watchtowers.
47. As has been said, when the emperor was victorious, he sat at supper together with Basil and Eudocia. Basiliscianus the patrician was praising the emperor for having driven the chariot so skillfully and cleverly. The emperor then immediately ordered him to rise, and, having stripped off his own red boots, bade him put them on. He refused, casting his eyes upon Basil. Which, however, the emperor, struck by anger, had commanded, and Basil nodded; so he put on the imperial boots. And the emperor says to Basil, swearing by God, "They suit him better." And so
At that time also, he promoted the aforementioned Nicholas, the *mansionarius* of Saint Diomedes, to the dignity of *oikonomos* and *syncellus*; he appointed his brother John as *drungarius* of the watch; he placed his other brother, Paul, in charge of the imperial *sakelle*; and finally, he created his third brother, Constantine, *logothete* of the public treasury.
11. In the ninth year of the reign of Basil, the emperor began to build the New Church, and the city of Syracuse was betrayed to the Saracens.
12. In the eleventh year of the reign of Basil, Nicetas Xylenites, the *praepositus* of the table, was accused of being loved by the empress and, having had his hair shorn, he was thrust into a monastery. The emperor melted down many bronze vessels for the sake of the New Church; he also took marble, mosaics, and columns from many churches and houses for its construction. Among these ornaments there was a bronze statue in the senate, wearing the habit of a bishop and holding a rod in its hand, around which a serpent was coiled. This statue was brought into the vestiary; when the emperor came and inserted his finger into the mouth of the serpent, a living snake that was hiding inside bit him, and he was so injured that he could barely be cured by any remedies. Furthermore, he took the statue of Solomon which was in the basilica, had it fashioned in his own name, and, offering himself to God as a sacrifice, he laid it in the foundations of the New Church.
13. In the eleventh year of his reign, the emperor again set out on an expedition against the Melitenians, and having been victorious in many battles and taken much booty and many captives, he returned.
14. In the twelfth year of his reign, the patriarch Ignatius died, and Photius was once again raised to the throne of the royal city.
15. In the thirteenth year of his reign, the emperor set out with his forces against Germanicia, and having plundered and devastated it, he returned. Constantine, the son of Michael by Eudocia—though in the opinion of the people he was held to be the son of Basil—died. Basil followed his funeral with great mourning and laid him to rest in the imperial tombs.
16. In the fourteenth year of his reign, on the Calends of May, the New Church was dedicated and opened for public divine worship by the patriarch Photius.
17. In the fifteenth year of his reign, Leo Salibaras introduced to the patriarch Photius the monk Theodore Santabarenos (who was later created archbishop of Euchaita by Photius), as a religious man and a wonderful worker of miracles, endowed with the art of foreknowing the future. Photius introduced the man to the emperor Basil; the emperor, delighted by him (since he assented to his desires), held him in great esteem. Seeing the emperor suffering from grief over the death of his son Constantine, through the strength of the love he bore him, this impostor promised that he would show him the boy alive; and in fact he did the deed. For as the emperor was riding past a certain thicket, a phantom appeared to him, sitting on a horse and wearing garments woven with gold, in the form of Constantine; he saw it with his eyes and embraced and kissed it.
John Curcuas, the Domestic of the Scholai, was plotting tyranny, since one who lived cloistered in the Blachernae had prophesied imperial power for him. There were sixty-six nobles who had conspired with him, among whom were Michael the Hetaireiarch, and Catudes, and Myxiares, and Babutzicus. Having seized them all, he had them beaten with rods in the Hippodrome, shaved their heads, burned what hair remained, and after parading them through the city, punished them with exile.
23. When the emperor had gone out for hunting, a stag of huge size appeared and lifted the emperor from his belt off his horse with its antlers. Someone arrived and, having cut the belt with his sword, set him free; but the emperor turned and cut off [that man's] head, saying, “Do not bare a sword against the emperor!” Having fallen ill from the jolting of the stag, the Emperor Basil died, having spoken these words to his son and successor Leo, and also to Stylianus, the guardian of his children: “The profane Photius and his fellow-initiate Santabarenus have drawn me far from God, and rendering me a stranger to righteous knowledge, have dragged me into the same abyss of punishment as themselves.” Having said these things, he gave up his soul, leaving behind Leo, Stephen, and Alexander.
Roman emperor Leo, 25 years, 9 months.
In the year of the world 6388, of the divine incarnation 888, the Roman emperor Leo reigned 25 years and 9 months. Stephen, his brother, being a cleric and syncellus, lived with Photius the patriarch, being raised and educated by him. But Leo, after becoming emperor, sent Andrew the strategelates with many wax tapers and clerics and men of the senatorial order to Chrysopolis, and brought back with great zeal, honor, and hymns the relics of the Emperor Michael, and deposited them in the church of the Holy Apostles in a sarcophagus. He expelled Photius the patriarch from the Church, after he was caught in treacherous activity, and confined him in the monastery of the Armeniacs, which they call Gordon; where he also died. He had Stephen, his own brother, ordained as syncellus by Theophanes, the prothronos, and the other bishops; and he lived 6 years and 5 months, and is buried in the monastery of the Sykai. A fire also occurred near the Hagia Sophia and the Iron [Gate], in which the church of Saint Thomas was burned down, which Leo himself magnificently restored. He brought Theodore Santabarenus from Euchaita into the city. But Andrew the domestic and Stephen the magistor brought charges against him and against Photius, because they had slanderously denounced the Emperor Leo to the Emperor Basil, his father. Upon the Senate’s passing judgment against them, they allowed Photius to remain at [the monastery of] Gordon, but they had Santabarenus beaten and exiled to Athens. Then, the emperor sending [men] after him, blinded him and exiled him to the East. But after many years, having been placated, he brought him [back] into the city and commanded him to receive an allowance from the New Church.
2. In the second year of his reign, owing to the sedition in Longobardia, Constantine, [the official] of the table, was sent with
6. In the ninth year of Emperor Leo, a conspiracy was formed against the Emperor by the kinsmen of Zautzas, and once it was uncovered, he seized them all, punished them with exile, and utterly destroyed the entire family of Zautzas.
7. In the tenth year of his reign, Antonios the patriarch died, and Nikolaos, the Emperor’s *mystikos*, was ordained patriarch. At that time, Demetrias was captured by Damian the Saracen. The Emperor crowned Anna, the daughter of Zoe, daughter of Zautzas, because there being no *Augusta*, he could not hold the banquets according to custom.
8. In the eleventh year of his reign, the Emperor married his third wife, Eudokia from the Opsikion, crowning her and naming her *Augusta*. She gave birth to a male child by him and died, and the infant that was born also died.
