9. In the thirteenth year of his reign, the Emperor built a temple in the place called the "Topoi" in the name of Saint Lazaros and constructed a monastery for eunuchs; there he deposited the body of Saint Lazaros and Saint Mary Magdalene. At this time, Tauromenion was surrendered to the Saracens.
10. In the fifteenth year, the island of Lemnos was taken by the Saracens.
11. In the sixteenth year, during the procession of Pentecost, as the Emperor was entering the church of Saint Mocios—which they say was formerly a temple of Hercules—when he had come near the *solea*, someone stepped out from the ambo and struck him on the head with a strong, thick staff. And had the blow not been hindered by the intervention of a many-branched suspended chandelier, he would have killed him on the spot. Because blood was flowing copiously from his head, terror seized the officials, and they fled. Because of this, and because his brother Alexander had feigned illness and not descended for the procession, a suspicion arose that he had been the author of such a conspiracy. The one who had struck the Emperor was seized and, after enduring many tortures and torments for many days, because he did not confess the name of any other person, his hands and feet were cut off and he was burned at the *meta* of the Hippodrome. From then on, such a procession was suppressed. Some time later, Marcus, the most wise *oikonomos* of that same church—who had completed the *tetraodion* of Lord Kosmas for the Great Week, that is, for Great Saturday—came to the Emperor, and while sharing a meal with him, he begged him not to abolish the feast and the procession. When the Emperor refused, Marcus said that this was foretold by David. He says, "How the enemy has acted wickedly in Your sanctuary, and those who hate You have boasted in the midst of Your feast" (Ps. LXXIII, 3). "And it is necessary, master, that from now on you hold the empire for ten years." And this is what happened, for on that same day on which he had received the blow, when the circle of those years had been completed, he died.
12. In the seventeenth year, the emperor married a fourth wife, Zoe, whom they call Carbonopsina.
13. In the eighteenth year, the naval army of the Hagarenes advanced against the city with the Tripolitan. They are called Tripolitans because they were gathered from three nations: from Arabs, Tyrians, and Sidonians who had migrated. Similarly, the Aegean Sea also received its name from the rush of its waters, leaping in the manner of a goat (which the Greeks call *aix*). Likewise, Strobolo was named from its local position, and Lampsacus from the brilliance of light, which shone down from heaven by night while the Phocaeans were laying foundations and praying; by this, through the favor of the divine, the stable framework of the walls was established. Imbrus was named from Imbrus, son of Antheus, whose father was Staphylus, the dearest offspring of Dionysus. The Hellespont [is named] from Helle, the sister of Phrixus, who was cast into the sea in that place. Cibyrrha [is named] from her brother Cibyrrhus. Thasos was formerly called Chryse. Samothracia, which is a peninsula in Thrace, was previously called Theriusa because it was full of wild beasts and was sacred to the Nymphs. Later, however, when it had been separated from the mainland by the collision of the current, it became an island and was possessed by Samian colonists; with its name changed, it was called Samothracia by them. Laodicea was named from the wife of Antiochus Seleucus, who founded Laodicea in her name. Tenedus is from Tenus, the son of Cycnus, son of Neptune, and from the temple located there dedicated to Minerva. Mesembria, previously Memnebria, [is named] from Memnos the Thracian, its founder, and *bria*, which among some Thracians means a city; but for the sake of smoother pronunciation, it is now called Mesembria. Haemus is from a certain person slain there by Hercules, by whose blood the place was stained; it is a promontory of Thrace. D Media obtained its name from Medis, daughter of Aeetes. Silybria [is named] from Silyus, king of the Thracians, who led a colony there. Macedonia, which was formerly called Amathia, located toward the sea, shares its name with the people. Nicopolis is named from the victory of Augustus over Antony and Cleopatra, by which he bent the Egyptian scepters to the Roman fasces. Hierum of the Euxine (as if one were to say "temple") was founded by the Argonauts as they traveled that way. Pharus is a structure of this kind, [a mound] upon which a torch is placed, so that travelers in the darkness of night may be guided without stumbling. The Russi, who by a congruent name are also called Dromitae, were so named from a certain strong man, Ros, because when they had escaped injuries and harm—either by warning and counsel, or by some divine inspiration and oracle, [or] through the power of those using them and those above them—they were called by this name; the speed of their running gave the cause for them to be called Dromitae. They derive their lineage from the Franks. The Tricephalus hill, which is in the Opsikion [theme], received its name from the figure by which it rises in a triple mound. Rhadenus [is named] from the town of Rhada in the Anatolic theme.
defiled by the sacred rites of Mahomet. But Constantine and the rest, knowing well that it was impossible for Andronicus to escape, soon after, by his advice, took the step of fleeing—a matter truly worthy of admiration—from the middle of Syria, and after being constantly exhausted by the enemy's detachments, they arrived in the territories of the Roman state.
23. At that time, the elder Abalbaces and the father of Samonas came to the city for the sake of an exchange. Having received them, the emperor showed them all the splendor of the church, though it was certainly not in accordance with the Christian rite, by which it is not fitting for those outside the faith to see the sacred vessels of God. The father of Samonas had the intention of remaining with his son and not returning to Syria. But Samonas discouraged him, saying: "Keep your faith; I prefer, if the opportunity allows, to come to you."
24. In the twenty-fifth year of his reign, on the day of Holy B Pentecost, the Emperor Leo crowned his son Constantine, who had been born in the purple, through the patriarch Euthymius. Now Samonas gave his own man, Constantine—who had previously served in the ministry of Basil the magistros and prefect of the kanikleion—to the service of Zoe Augusta. This man became very dear to the Emperor Leo and to the Empress. For this reason, Samonas, being kindled with great envy toward him, accused him of having a clandestine affair with the Empress. Leo, suspecting that the accusation was true and wishing to do a favor for Samonas, ordered Constantine to have his hair tonsured in the monastery of Saint Tarasius. Yet, a short time after, Samonas ordered him to receive him again in his own monastery, which they call Spira, wishing to restore him to his office. The emperor set out, and after holding a procession to Damatrys, and having dined in the monastery of Samonas, he saw Constantine. And at once he ordered Samonas to have him dressed in secular garments, and he mingled with the emperor at the banquet, and after this he returned to the palace. And again, the envy of Samonas blazed up, because of the benevolence with which the emperor treated Constantine. Having entered into a plot with Tzirithon and certain others, they drew up a document full of abuse against the emperor, having it written and composed by the Rhodian, who was the notary of Samonas. When the emperor came to the Great Church, he threw it down in the Metatorion. The emperor, being offended upon reading the pamphlet, was anxious and grieved, searching for the one who had done this. It happened then that the moon was eclipsed, and the emperor ordered the astronomers who were in the city at the time to declare what that eclipse portended. Now, when Pantaleon the metropolitan entered to the emperor, Samonas asked him: "Against whom is this malice directed?" He replied: "Against you, indeed; and if the thirteenth of the month of June passes, you will suffer nothing more serious." But to the Emperor Leo he said that the moon’s affliction concerned him who was second in the state; which the emperor took to mean his brother Alexander. Afterwards, Tzirithon said privately to the emperor that Samonas was the author of the pamphlet. And immediately he deposed him from his rank in his own house
they arrived at the Hippodrome gate, acclaiming Constantine as emperor with auspicious cries. There his groom, being struck by a lance by those inside the Hippodrome gates, was killed. Constantine was not received therein, but was repelled and driven away; as if possessed by some demon and not having his reason sound due to his lust for the empire, he withdrew from the Hippodrome, sad and downcast, judging the slaughter of his groom to be a bad omen. From there he came as far as the Chalke; and having entered through the Iron Gate of the Chalke, he reached the quarters of the Skoutaroi [the guards]. Then the Magister John Helladas, having made a selection from the Company and others, sent them armed against the Doux. When they had arrived at the Chalke and the two sides had clashed, many from both sides fell, so many that the place was engulfed in blood. Gregoras, the son of the Doux, was also killed, as was Michael, his cousin, and that Curtices who was of the Armenian race. When Constantine the Doux learned of these things, in the midst of a great tumult, he urged his horse into the crowd; but the animal, having slipped on the pavement that had been made slippery there, cast its rider to the ground. And someone cut off his head with a sword; this was presented to the emperor, manifest to all for the turning aside of the deception.
3. Now such an enterprise of his was revealed in yet another way. For a certain Nicolaus, who had been entrusted with the collection of public taxes in Chaldia, had arrived in Syria, having abjured the Christian piety and faith, and applied himself to astrology, or rather to astronomy. This man wrote on a black-dyed cloth and sent it to the Logothete Thomas; when it was thrown into water by Manuel, the interpreter of the Hagarenes, the letters written upon it appeared, with the meaning conceived in these words: "Do not fear the red bird [the Doux]; for he will foolishly desire new things, and will immediately perish."
4. After these things were accomplished in this way, Gregoras, his father-in-law, together with Leo whom they called Choerosphaktes, fled to the Church of the Holy Wisdom of God; from there they were dragged by force and tonsured according to the rite of the monks in the Monastery of Stoudios. As for Constantine the Helladic, they beat him with ox-hide cords, clothed him in a hair-shirt, set him upon an ass, and paraded him through the middle of the city for the sake of ignominy, and shut him away in the Dalmata prison to undergo discipline. And some they blinded and sent into exile, and others they beheaded. As for the soldiers, men who were brave and upright, they impaled them all in huge numbers from Damalis [that is, "the Heifer"] to the Leucatium. And the so-called guardians would have destroyed many others as well, had not some of the judges held them back from their unjust impulse, saying, "Since the emperor is a child, how do you dare to carry out such things without his command?"
And so, after shearing the wife of the Doux and castrating his son, they sent them off to their own house in Paphlagonia.
5. In the month of August, Symeon the Bulgar, with a numerous and strong army, made for Byzantium and, having led a rampart from sea to sea, he trusted greatly that he would take the city. But, having been cast down in his hopes by the countless multitude of citizens, the violence of their arms, and the protection of the walls, he requested peace treaties and returned to the Hebdomon. When the regents most willingly consented to the peace, Symeon and his children, having been treated with immense and very great gifts, returned to their own land.
6. Since the emperor Constantine was a child and was inquiring for his own mother (for Alexander had already removed her from the palace), she was recalled to him. Once she had gained control of the palace and the empire, she brought Constantine the *parakoimomenos*, and Constantine and Anastasius the Gongylioi, into the palace; and on the advice of John Helladas, she cast out the men of the emperor Alexander—namely, John the rector, Gabrielopoulos, and the rest. Zoe Augusta appointed Theophylact, the son of Domenicus, as *hetæriarch*. However, the *magister* John Helladas was seized by disease and passed away. Furthermore, at the prompting of the *hetæriarch* Theophylact, Zoe cast Patriarch Nicholas out of the palace with anger, saying that he should care for and tend to his own Church.
Not long after, Constantine the *parakoimomenos* accused the *hetæriarch* to Zoe, claiming he was plotting to transfer the sovereignty into the hands of his brother. Therefore, having feigned that he was made a *patrician*, when he went down to the church to receive a blessing as was the custom, she commanded him to remain at home. Zoe then appointed John the Garidas as *hetæriarch*, and the eunuch Damian as *droungarios* of the Watch. C
7. While the Bulgar Symeon was ravaging Thrace, John Bogas requested that he be elevated to the dignity of *patrician* and bring the Patzinaks to his aid; having received gifts, he went off and brought hostages into the city, with the Patzinaks agreeing to cross over and destroy Symeon by an attack.
8. Asotius also arrived in the city, a man most renowned for his strength, being the son of the Prince of Princes. They say that when he held an iron rod by its ends in his hands, because of his surpassing might he would bend it and twist it into a circular shape, the nature of the iron, though resistant, yielding to the force of his hands. Having arrived, he was received with great honor and respect, and they sent him back again to his own land.
9. In the month of September, in the third indiction, Pankratoukas the Armenian betrayed Adrianople to Symeon. Not long after, Basil the *patrician* was sent with many gifts and received it back. Damian also came...
pedagogue and his brother Symeon by Theophylact the patrician and Count of the Stable; and while they were eating, John Curcuas, the Drungary of the Watch, entered with a numerous band of attendants, and having seized them—as if they were conspiring against Romanus—he banished them to the Opsikion theme.
17. On the twenty-fourth day of the month of September, Romanus is honored with the dignity of Caesar; and on the seventeenth day of the month of December in the year 6458, he is crowned with the imperial crown by the Emperor Constantine and the Patriarch Nicholas. On the day of the holy Theophania [Epiphany], Theodora, his wife, is crowned Augusta. And on the same day of the same month, of the eighth indiction, Christophorus the son of Romanus is declared emperor; he is also proclaimed Augustus by the Emperor Constantine on the holy day of Pentecost; and the two of them proceeded alone in state through the city.
18. In the month of July of the same indiction, the union of those who were in schism took place.
19. In the month of October of the ninth indiction, Stephanus the Master, having fallen under suspicion of aspiring to the imperial throne, was shorn [as a monk] on the island of Antigoni together with his men, Theophanes Ticheotes and Paul the Orphanotrophos. Another conspiracy was then reported to be plotted by Arsenius and Paul of the Manglabitai; who, having been scourged and their property confiscated, were sent into exile.
20. Rentakes the Helladic, having become a parricide, since he had plundered all his father’s goods and fled to the city, prepared there to flee to Bulgaria with forged documents he had drawn up. He was captured, therefore, and blinded.
21. And again the Bulgarians make incursions as far as Katasyrtai after the death of the Duke Adralestes. Pothus, the son of Argyrus, the Domestic of the Schools, was appointed in his stead; and having led an army as far as Thermopolis, he sent Michael, the son of Marolis, to scout the Bulgarian camp. He, having fallen unexpectedly upon their wedge of troops, slew many of the Bulgarians, but he himself was wounded and, having been carried into the city, he died.
22. A conspiracy is again reported against Romanus, which was to have been carried out by Anastasius the Sakellarios, Theodoretus the Cubicularius, Demetrius the protonotary of the Eudikon, and certain others, as if they were supposedly struggling on behalf of the Emperor Constantine. They were convicted, scourged, and, having been led through the middle of the city, were sent into exile.
23. Romanus, using this as a pretext, demotes the Emperor Constantine and places him second to himself. Again, therefore, the Bulgarians make an excursion as far as the Manglaba. Emperor Romanus, having learned of this, sends John the Rector together with Leo and Pothus the Argyroi, having with them a sufficient multitude from both the tagmata and the federates, to receive their attack. One...
to be seen a soul that was truly imperial and magnanimous, and one had to marvel at his fearless spirit and the stature of his courage, that, seeing such an onslaught of enemies, he was not struck with dread nor did he shrink or retreat, but just as if he were entering into a throng of friendly lands, he proceeded so calmly, appearing to offer his own life to the enemy as a ransom for the salvation of his subjects. Therefore, having arrived first at the stated landing-stage, he awaited Symeon. When, however, hostages were taken from both sides and the Bulgarians had searched the landing-stage thoroughly, lest any trickery or ambush might be hidden there, Symeon dismounted from his horse and entered to the emperor. Having therefore greeted one another, they began conversations concerning peace. And it is said that the emperor spoke to Symeon as follows: "I have heard that you are a man who worships God and is a true Christian, yet I see your deeds in no way according with the reports and your words. For it is the mark of a man who worships God and is a Christian to cherish peace and love, since God is and is called love, whereas it is the mark of a godless and faithless man to rejoice in slaughter and blood unjustly poured forth. If, therefore, you are a true Christian, as we firmly believe, put an end at last to this unjust slaughter and the shedding of unholy blood, and strike a pact of peace with us Christians, being and being called a Christian yourself, and do not wish to stain the right hands of Christians with the blood of fellow-Christian believers. You are yourself a man, awaiting death and resurrection and judgment and retribution, existing today, only to be dissolved into dust tomorrow. A single fever will extinguish all the haughtiness of a proud spirit. What account, therefore, will you render to God for these unjust slaughters when you have departed from human life? With what countenance will you gaze upon the terrible and just Judge? If the desire for riches agitates you, I shall sate you with them until you have your fill. Only hold back your right hand, embrace peace, and cherish harmony, so that you yourself may live a life that is peaceful, bloodless, and free from trouble, and so that the sorrows and deaths of Christians may finally come to an end. For it is a sin that they should take up arms against those who worship the same faith."
30. Having said these things, the emperor fell silent. Symeon, however, having been moved to reverence by such great humility and such words, gave his assent to establish peace. Having therefore greeted one another and separated, the emperor treated Symeon with magnificence, presenting him with noble gifts. What happened then, I shall narrate, for it was a prodigy and a thing new and unheard-of, at least in the opinion and judgment of those who know how to discern such things. They say that two eagles flew down from above while the sovereigns were talking together, and that they shrieked and grappled with one another and then immediately parted, one flying toward the city and the other toward Thrace.
paper and ink. They brought him a new tome, and down near the end, they had him write: "I, Tryphon, by the mercy of God monk and patriarch, finding what is written above acceptable and giving my assent to it, have signed with my own hand." Having received this, they consecrate him patriarch. Then they write above the signature of the patriarch Tryphon whatever they themselves wished; and after the completion of the stated time, they depose him, although against the patriarch's will, yet persuaded by his own signature and unable to bear the deception. This was done because Theophylact, the son of the Emperor Romanus, was now reaching the required age and appeared mature enough for the priesthood.
38. On the twenty-fifth day of the same month, there was a winter so unbearable that the earth did not thaw from the frost for one hundred and twenty days. From this, a great famine and much mortality resulted, so that the living were not sufficient to carry out the dead. Wherefore, the Emperor Romanus, having pondered that unbearable force of the cold and desiring to alleviate the want caused by the famine, blocked off the spaces of the porticos with boards and installed windows, so that the snow and the cold might not enter in upon the poor from those places; and he constructed boxes in all the porticos. He ordered that silver should be given monthly to the poor taking shelter in these, and that quarterly allowances be distributed to the poor in the churches, so that the total distributed each month reached twelve thousand stamped silver pieces. Three poor men ate with him daily, each receiving one coin. On Wednesdays and Fridays, he likewise brought in monks, who each received one coin. There was also one of the boys who read at his table. Being a lover of monks, if anyone else were led by such an affection, he would pour out into tears when speaking of his actions to all the men who were zealously striving for virtue. He provided ornamentation of every kind for the churches; he sent gifts and stipends to the monks exercising their duties on the mountains everywhere. He cherished the monasteries and, for those who had cut their hair for God in them or were even recluses, he ceased not to provide established stipends. We have recounted these things as a few out of many of his countless deeds of accomplishment and almsgiving.
39. John, the brother of Peter the Bulgar, conspired against him along with other grandees of Symeon. When they were discovered, John was beaten and shut up in prison, while the others were subjected to no common punishments. When the emperor learned of this, he sent and brought John to himself.
40. On the second day of the month of March, a man named Kosmites fell in the Forum from among the columns standing in order there, and he crushed six men. A fire also occurred at the portico of the Theotokos of the Forum, and the wax-chandlery and furriers' shops were burned as far as the Pychai.
41. In the month of August, of the fourth indiction,
Emperor Christophorus passed away and was buried in the house of his father. Patriarch Tryphon also, having fulfilled the appointed time in his office, was removed and ended his life in his own monastery. The Church remained widowed of a pastor for one year and five months, because of the youth of Theophylact, the son of Romanus.
42. A certain imposter, Basilius the Macedonian, falsely claiming to be Constantine Ducas, enticed many to follow him. Having been seized, he had one of his hands cut off. Then, finding an opportunity, he returned to the Opsikion; having fitted in its place an artificial hand made of bronze and fashioned an oversized sword, he went about deceiving many, pretending he was Constantine Ducas. Having seized a fortress called "Flat Rock" (Lata Petra) and gathered a great store of provisions, he stirred up a great rebellion against the Roman state, plundering those who fell into his hands and ravaging the province. But when he was captured again, he was brought into the city and they burned him in the Forum of the Amastrianus.
43. In the month of February, of the fourth indiction, on the feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Hypapante), the aforementioned son of the emperor, Theophylact, was ordained patriarch, when vicars arrived from Rome bringing a synodical tome C prescribing his ordination; by them he was also installed upon the patriarchal throne. Romanus the emperor took for his son Stephen a wife, Anna, the daughter of Gamalas, who, together with the nuptial crown, was also adorned with the imperial diadem.
44. In the month of April, of the seventh indiction, occurred the first incursion of the Turks against the Roman territory; they overran as far as the city and ravaged all the Thracian populace. The patricius and protovestiarius Theophanes was therefore sent, and he made a peace agreement with them.
45. On the fourteenth of the month of January, Romanus took a wife for his youngest son, Constantine, from the race of the Armeniacs, namely Helen, the daughter of the patricius Hadrian; and she having died, in the second month of February he wed him to another wife, from the family of that Mamos, named Theophano.
46. In that same year, the Russians, also called Dromitai (as if one were to say "the wanders" and "expeditious in movement"), originating from the Frankish race, came with a fleet of ten thousand ships to attack Constantinople. The protovestiarius Theophanes was therefore sent against them with naval forces, and having drawn up the fleet, he withstood their attack. Now when they had reached the Pharos in the Euxine Pontus (a "Pharos" is a mole or tower upon which a flame is lit to guide travelers by night; the Euxine Pontus is so called by antiphrasis, as if it were "hospitable," for it was known as "inhospitable" on account of the constant outrages of the robbers there against travelers; once these were destroyed by Hercules, the travelers, having gained security, renamed it the "Euxine")—in the place they call Hieron, which means temple (a name it received because those sailing in the Argo
and having explicitly secured this with them, that if they should in any way transgress against the first emperor or violate his majesty, they should be immediately deposed from the empire, he shamefully drove him out of the palace, and having exiled him to the island of Prote, he punished him with the monastic tonsure.
The Empire of Constantine Porphyrogenitus
A 753 1. The emperor Constantine, his son-in-law, was therefore left as emperor on the twentieth day of the month of December, of the third indiction, in the year 6454. He immediately created Bardas Phocas magistrate and Domestic of the Schools, and Constantine commander of the naval forces, as well as certain other energetic and industrious men as tribunes. He made Petinus patrician and Count of the Stable, and Curtice patrician and Drungary of the Watch; for whom divine vengeance followed shortly after, in that they had behaved wickedly against the Lord's anointed. For, being caught in an act of high treason, both departed this life by a miserable death.
2. After a space of forty days, Constantine Porphyrogenitus, suspecting his brothers Stephen and Constantine, lest they should attempt the same things against him—as was reasonable, for if they had not spared their own father, how would they spare him?—what, then, becomes of his brethren? It is said, for: "Evil communications corrupt good manners." Having received them at a banquet and feasted them sumptuously, even while the food was not yet swallowed, those who were prepared for this seized them and, having enrolled them in the clerical order, deported them to islands not far away. Shortly thereafter, they sought to visit their father, who was a monk, being affected by unendurable grief at the sight. And the father likewise, lamenting, said to them: "I have reared and brought up children, and they have rebelled against me." From there they were exiled, Stephen indeed to Proconnesus, then to Rhodes, afterwards to Mitylene, and Constantine to Tenedos, and from thence to Samothrace, where he even plotted tyranny and was killed by his guards. Michael, the son of the emperor Christopher, having been stripped of his imperial sandals, was made a cleric by the patriarch Theophylact. Likewise, he also sent into exile the patrician and *accubitor* Theophanes, as well as George the protospatharios and pincerna, and Thomas the primicerius; all of whom, having been subjected to beating and tonsured, he paraded through the middle of the city in an infamous triumph and sent off into exile. And thus notice was taken of them, because they were found to be seizing an opportunity to bring Romanus back into the palace again.
3. In the month of December, of the sixth indiction, a plot having been formed to bring Stephen back from the island into the palace, since it was reported by Michael Diabolinos, all were captured; some were mutilated by having their noses and ears cut off, and others were subjected to intolerable beatings, and having been placed upon donkeys for the sake of ignominy, they were paraded through the city and sent off into exile.
th indiction, in the year 6456, leaving as emperor his son Romanos, together with his mother, Helena.
Roman emperor Romanos, 3 years, 3 months, 5 days.
In the year of the world 6456, of the divine incarnation 956, the Roman emperor Romanos [reigned for] 3 years, 3 months, 5 days. He was a young man in age, vigorous in body, of wheat-colored complexion, [P. 497] bright-eyed, with an aquiline nose, of pleasing appearance, sweet of speech, upright as a cypress tree, broad in the shoulders, quiet and gentle. Left as emperor by his father, Constantine Porphyrogenitus, at the age of 21, along with his son Basil, who was one year old, his mother Helena, and his own wife Theophano, he immediately promoted his father's household members and courtiers to greater honors and dignities, and after enriching them with generous gifts, he expelled them from the palace. He chose and appointed as associates for the administration of public affairs and as leaders of the senate Joseph the patrician and drungary of the fleet, whom he shortly after made *accubitor* (that is, prefect of the sacred bedchamber and of all domestic affairs); John the protospatharios, whom they call Chounos, he appointed patrician and grand hetaireiarch; Sisinnios the protospatharios, who was *exsacellarios*, he appointed prefect of the city, and shortly after patrician and logothete of the drome. His sisters, Zoe, Theodora, Agatha, Theophano, and Anna, he drove out of the royal halls and transferred to the royal residence called the Kanikleion, where Sophia, the wife of the emperor Christopher, already resided in tonsure; after a few days he assigned them to the house of Antiochus, and sent Agatha to the monastery founded by his grandfather Romanos, decreeing that the same expenses they received in the palace should be provided to them. He honored the domestic of the schools and patrician Nicephorus Phocas with the title of *magistros* and sent him to the East. Similarly, he also made his brother, the patrician Leo, *magistros* and domestic of the West. He himself was entirely given over to hunting and the chase, living a life of luxury. He also fathered another son after the death of his father, whom he named Constantine; he was soon crowned by the patriarch Polyeuctos on the ambo of Hagia Sophia. But Augusta Helena, having stayed in the palace with the emperor, eventually fell ill and died on the 19th of the month of September. She was buried in the monastery of her father, the emperor Romanos, and laid in a sarcophagus near him.
2. The emperor sent the *magistros* and domestic Nicephorus Phocas, with a great army and a heavy force of warships and Greek fire, against Crete. Crete had formerly been under Roman rule, until, during the reign of Michael the Stammerer, father of Theophilus, it was captured by Saracens from Spain, while the Roman fleet was meanwhile occupied in suppressing the tyranny and rebellion of the foolish Thomas, who had been a companion and fellow-soldier of [Michael] before
a man—though I know not if it is fitting to call him a man, and not rather a beast, and one more savage and bitter than the beasts. For what is more pitiable than this catastrophe, and what is more wretched than this misfortune? What a great evil has encroached upon the empire! In birth he was doubly-born and mixed-barbarian, in character multifaceted, ignoble, and in his mindset unstable—so far as this concerns his immediate kin and that which is commonly known.
11. For he was indeed a native of the Armenian land, from which source he derived that depravity and malignancy of morals. But there is another more remote and ancient account, as certain elders relate who know what has long been handed down concerning him and his ancestors, nor does he trace his birth purely to these, if it be true that he stemmed from a most wretched offshoot of the Assyrian race, from whom rumor says he derived his paternal blood. For he was descended from the children of Sennacherib, who once ruled over the Assyrians and received a mortal wound from his own offspring. For those who raised their murderous right hands against their own father, being defiled with parricidal gore and having become with their own hands the slaughterers of their begetter, immediately fled and found refuge in the territories of the Armenians. From this parricidal seed, preserved through evil fate until our own generation, this fruit of murder—I know not by what divine judgment this evil was assigned to the Roman empire—was born into this light; he who is familiar with the things he knows, guided by wicked and destructive judgments, is reared and increases in the same customs and deeds.
12. From the Assyrian lioness and the Armenian leopard, therefore, a cross-bred and compound and unnatural kind of tiger was born from a bastard and spurious lineage—whether Armenian or Assyrian, more hideous [to behold]. He was given the name of Leo, but he possessed that rapacious strength of a lion for his own security, while in no way adopting its noble qualities. As for the chameleon, as he was in appearance, so also was he in the multifaceted nature of his soul, because of the deceitful and treacherous character of his lineage, from which he was fostered in that hidden and unspeakable malice and guile. For he schemed to keep hidden the darkness and double-dealing that lurked within his mind, and his inclination toward harming others. And how he had labored and practiced himself never to let his heart agree with his lips, so that he might meditate one thing in his mind while uttering other things with his mouth! But indeed, his life had drifted to such a degree of godlessness and wickedness, that even the demonic [nature] of his mind and his wickedness were difficult for all to perceive and observe.
19. By what reasoning, therefore, will the unbelieving and ungrateful turn away their eyes, beholding what even to this day is celebrated and seen as a miracle in the temple of the holy Mother of God, in the city they call Lydda? The apostles erected this sacred building while the blessed Virgin was still surviving in this life. For many have seen what is held and worshipped with veneration—that venerable image not made by hands, expressed upon shining and pellucid tablets, and extending through the whole depth and fullness. Indeed, when certain of the hostile Greeks and Jews attempted to scrape it away out of hatred for Christ, they vanished, their absurd attempt frustrated. For the more they scraped the effigy, the more it shone with greater light and a brighter ray. It remained nevertheless in the same form and habit. The appearance of the face and the clothing, and whatever else strikes the eyes, remained integral to it, not forgotten nor marred by any stain. For they relate that when the holy apostles, relying on the promise of the most holy Mother of God, had erected a temple under the invocation of her name, and she herself, at their request, had entered the temple for the purpose of sanctifying it and had stood by one of the columns, an image of her was found expressed upon the column; which, as has been said, the Greeks and Jews, despite applying force, could not scrape away.
20. Moreover, a witness besides other divine things is the statue which the woman suffering from an issue of blood—by whom she had been cured—set up near her own house to Christ as a monument of gratitude; a most manifest title and herald of the truth most sincere, exhibiting a miracle wrought in her own person, an unheard-of divine sign and prodigy, spreading it far into future generations; concerning which also the great master among those who fashion images, Eusebius, having made mention in his Ecclesiastical History of the miracles performed by the sacred herb which grew there from a divine stock, and how it was a present help and an expeller of evils for all, adds, saying that he had seen the images of the apostles Peter and Paul, and of Christ himself, painted with a brush and colors, which were still in existence.
21. But sight is also a witness, a truthful teacher; and the length of time clearly shows it, as does the construction of the venerable sacred buildings. Furthermore, there is the creation and consecration of the vessels of sacred ministry, effected in ancient times by the pious zeal of the faithful; of which there is a nearly infinite multitude and abundance. There are also the covers of the holy Gospels, which bear sacred forms and figures, exposed to the veneration of all the faithful and worshipped by them. Likewise the divine columns, and what they call the soles, which partition off the venerable holy sanctuary. For indeed, to no one...
a steward of the palace, himself a kinsman of Michael. These men, having armed themselves by night and carrying swords concealed beneath their cloaks, entered the church in the manner of priests, with the steward having opened the way. And when the emperor had entered the church, they rushed upon him and took away his most wicked and impious soul, in the palace, where none of the emperors before him had been slain.
25. Furthermore, at that very hour, they went into the prison, brought out Michael, and led him into the festival, adorned with imperial robes (that is to say, his head wreathed with a diadem) instead of chains, so that the oracle of the Psalmist might be fulfilled in him: *Weeping shall remain for the evening, and in the morning there shall be rejoicing.* (Ps. XXIX, 6). But afterward, these most wicked men covered his limbs with cheap rags and, having cast his most impure corpse into a small boat, they carried the funeral to the island which they call Prote, and buried him there. In that very place, they tonsured his children and committed them to the ways of life instituted for monks. I do not know for what reason this place was singled out to receive such a stain in place of such great evils.
26. Moreover, one must observe that if the doctrine of these impious and infamous men—and the things which they devise—had possessed any semblance of truth, it would certainly have been preserved and known in other provinces as well. But now, it is nowhere to be seen in the world, that which they so hold. For at the same time, both East and West venerate this evangelical and apostolic preaching and have received it into their practice; and wherever, in any region under the name of Christians, that wicked and corrupt sprout of Christian piety, that dogma hated by God, is heard of, they do not even allow into their minds the memory of those most base and execrable books, except only in this enslaved, downtrodden nation which plays with divine things at its own whim; in which, indeed, licentiousness and the love of earthly things, to linger and wallow on the earth, the earthly mind, and the spirit swollen with pride and arrogance, prevail. Where that blind and carnal love of self, that ruined desire for power, pleasures, and glory reside, to enjoy all things from the heart, to wish to be superior to all, and to be unwilling to be subject even to God Himself; the acquisition and pursuit of human glory, or to speak more truly, of confusion. Hence the...
for they burned most of his ships and routed the select men of his army. A Thomas, however, seized by helplessness, left the city and proceeded into Thrace to plunder it. Having plundered it, then, to a certain degree—though not as he had hoped, but as divine providence permitted on account of our sins—he accomplished nothing more of what he had expected in his mind. And so, not only did his endeavor turn out contrary to him and end in failure, but he was also stripped of the fame and strength which he seemed to possess and with which he flattered himself: it is almost as if one might say he had lost everything.
6. The citizens, as has been said, having arrayed themselves powerfully and bravely, and with the divine power and help and the hope of that same race, joining in battle from the walls, by land, and by sea, with strong vigor and constancy of labor, broke almost all of his rotting boastfulness and pride. For they burned with fire those fortifications—I mean to say, his ships and fleet—in which, having foolishly trusted, he was excessively proud and arrogant; and having routed and put to flight the select men of the enemy and their commanders, a great vertigo and helplessness held the unstable and most fickle Thomas. Furthermore, all his manifold and cunning engines, which he thought were unassailable and difficult to capture, they easily and readily rendered useless and destroyed. And in this way, the house of David (that is, the City of God) indeed proceeded and grew strong, while the house of Saul, the troop of the enemy, went on and was truly weakened, and God was glorified. And one could clearly see the holy oracle being fulfilled in him: "You have broken down all his hedges; You have turned his strongholds into terror," and the rest of the Psalm. Therefore, the people of the city also sang: "Some in chariots, and some in horses," and the rest of the Psalm.
7. The divine word, acting as a sponsor and giving them a blessed foretelling of the sudden and most rapid downfall of their enemy, replied—saying little less than this: "Take heart, O children, and cry out to God; for there shall be for you by Him a remembrance of Him who is the avenger." For He who brought upon you these evils, He Himself, along with salvation, has brought to you eternal joy.
8. Since, therefore, the wretched and miserable man, exhausted and misled by his own mind, was fighting foolishly and laboring in vain, and was quite...
and slaughtered an infinite multitude of people. But indeed their fleets also went out and ravaged the Cyclades islands, and made Crete and Sicily subject to them. There was also a huge cold and a most savage and bitter winter, and a strong famine, and droughts of the air, and intemperances and irregularities, and terrible and frequent earthquakes, which were things proving the immeasurable wickedness and evil-doing of the one ruling. For the impious man had sunk into such godlessness and madness that, having accepted and renewed the heavy and most abominable heresy of Copronymus and those with bestial names, he appeared no less in his impiety and licentiousness, his tyranny and senselessness, than that deluded and foolish-minded man. Since, therefore, he was caught in the same error and stupidity and frenzy as those impious and most wicked men, driven by Manichaean madness and Arian rage, he himself also similarly extended persecution against the Church.
4. To this man—or rather, I should say "Misotheus" (God-hater), rather than the name that sounds the opposite, Theophilus—there came as a refugee the Persian Theophobus, together with his father and fourteen thousand Persians, whom he distributed among the provinces, settling them and establishing them in *turmai*, which to this day are called the "Persian *turmai*." And he made Theophobus himself his kinsman, by giving him the sister of Theodora Augusta as his wife.
5. This same Theophilus, through the head of the goldsmiths, a man of great learning and skill and a kinsman of Anthony the Patriarch, constructed what they call the Pentapyrgion, and two very large organs made of solid gold and decorated with various stones and pearls; as well as a golden tree, in which birds sitting upon it sang musically by means of a certain mechanism. The emperor also renewed the imperial robes, restoring and fashioning with gold-weaving what they call the *lora*, and all other things.
6. Pretending to possess worldly justice, while in fact having wronged the faith and piety more than those who had reigned before him, a widow woman approached him in Blachernae (for it was his custom to go there) and cried out that she was being wronged by Petrona, the brother of the Augusta, who was the *droungarios* of the Watch: "For he is raising up his own houses with the new buildings he is constructing, and he is casting shadows over mine and making them useless, as I am but a despised widow." He immediately sent Eustathios the quaestor, nicknamed "the Monk," who lived in Oxeia, together with Leo Symbatios and Demetrios D Camulianos, to inspect whether the new
aedifices of the widow against justice and law. llbracketA Depart-
ing therefore, and having seen such an injury, and having
verified that the things which had been said and charged
by the woman were true, when they had returned, they
reported it to the emperor; and Petronas having been
convicted by them in the eyes of the emperor, with his
garments stripped off, he was severely beaten on his
back. It was further decreed that the quaestor and the
particular receivers or guards, departing, should raze
such buildings to the ground and restore them to the
woman.
7. He took to himself as a son-in-law Alexius the Armenian,
surnamed Musele, a man of strong vigor and energetic,
having betrothed to him his beloved daughter Maria, and
created him patrician, and shortly after also magister.
But later, because he had come under suspicion of having
aspired to the empire and as if he were plotting something
against him, he sent him away as master of the militia and
commander of Sicily. But what manner of thing does
envy beget! Certain Sicilians, going up to the emperor,
laid a calumny against him, as if he were betraying the
affairs of the Christians to the Saracens and against
the emperor was stirring up revolutionary plots. Inter
these events, Maria, the most beloved daughter of
Theophilus, having been taken from the living, he
adorned her urn with silver, and placed a scroll of liberty
and immunity for those who might flee to it for any
crime; which silver Emperor Leo afterwards took away
from the tomb.
8. Further, Theophilus, summoning Theodorus the bishop,
called the Crithinus, who was at that time staying in the
city when Alexius was attacked by calumny, and having
delivered to him a pectoral cross, sent him to Sicily to
offer the sworn pledge of immunity to Alexius and to
bring him to the city to the emperor. He departed, and
by the prudence and sagacity of mind with which he was
endowed, having persuaded Alexius and having found him
amenable, he brought him to the emperor. The emperor,
as soon as he had returned, as if he had attempted tyranny
and was a rebel, having afflicted him with blows and
bound him, and having confiscated his entire estate,
threw him into prison. Then Theodorus said to Alexius:
I am the cause that you suffer all these dire things. C 
And the emperor having departed to Blachernae according
to custom, he himself, anticipating him, stood inside
the place of the sacred tribunal, clothed in priestly vest-
ment; and as the emperor approached the soleas, the
senate surrounding him, the archbishop called out with a
loud voice: "Bend thy bow, and proceed prosperously,
and reign," for what cause, O king? The emperor, there-
fore, revering the senate, said: "For the sake of truth and
meekness and justice." [Ps. XLIV, 5.] And he: What
kind of justice is in you, seeing that you have not kept
the word which you gave by me as a sworn pledge to
Alexius? The emperor, not bearing the reproach and
inflamed by that anger, violently expelled him from the
sacred place of the altar and, having inflicted not
moderate blows upon him, relegated him to exile; and
not for that reason alone because he had been reproached
by him, but because he had heard that he honored and
venerated the holy icons and denounced his impiety. But
not long after, when the emperor had arrived at the Great
Church, and the patriarch had rebuked him for the affair,
he brought the archbishop himself back with requests.
And since he judged himself unworthy of the priesthood
on account of what he had suffered, the emperor
Blachernae on some business, someone approached him and said: "The horse upon which your majesty is riding is mine." Since the horse began to leap on account of the sudden encounter, the emperor held him in and asked the count of the stable: "Whose is this horse?" He replied: "The Count of the Opsikion sent him to your majesty." When this was discovered in the city, the next day the emperor brought him forward and said to the man who had approached him: "Speak the truth, whose is the horse?" The man answered and said: "It was mine, and the general sent for it and took it from me by force, giving me neither payment nor dignity." The emperor said to the count: "Tell me if this is so, and why, without settling the matter regarding the horse, you sent him to me." He replied: "He was seeking to become a scholaris; but I, not knowing whether he was a brave man, offered him a hundred nomismata, but he refused to take them." The emperor said: "And why did you not complete a full bargain with him regarding the price before sending the horse to me?" After the emperor had investigated and was fully informed that he had taken it by force, he disciplined the general with the appropriate scourging, and returned the horse to the man who had approached him. However, he did not wish to receive the horse, but instead took two litrai for it, and the general was ordered to test him, and if he were a brave man, to make him a scholaris. But when he went to war, in the heat of battle he was found to be a coward among the fugitives and was cut down by the enemies.
22. When the emperor was at Bryantas for a procession, a message came to him from the general of the Anatolics that the protosymbolos had gone out with a multitude and was moving to capture Amorium. The emperor, having done what was customary for the army and the commanders, set out quickly as far as Cappadocia. The Amerumnes, having detached fifty thousand of his men—having drawn them from the rest of the army—and having appointed as their leader and head Sudaes, a man most renowned among the Agarenes for his reputation for bravery and prudence, sent them against the emperor. A battle was joined, and the emperor, having been defeated, fled; he returned in shame, having barely escaped safely. The Amerumnes, however, having gone off with a great force, surrounded Amorium with a wall, and though he waged many wars, he was unable to capture it, as those inside fought bravely and steadfastly. A certain student of Leo the Philosopher was in the fortress, and since he wished to defect to the Amerumnes, the astronomer indicated to him, through someone, that: "If you persevere for two days at the fortress, you will capture us." And this is what happened, for it was betrayed by those called Boiditzes and Manicophagus. The named men, who were not ignoble, were seized and captured and taken off as prisoners to Syria: Theophilus the patrician and general, and Melissenus, and Aetius, and Theodorus the protospatharios, the eunuch Krateros, and...
as well as money and estates, naming it after Theophilus.
29. Before his own death, this God-hating emperor, having held a secret council with his associates concerning Theophobus the Persian, since the latter was, of course, very greatly beloved, and since both the Persian subjects and not a few of the nobles had faith in him, and fearing lest after my death they might plot a tyranny against my son, who is still a child, and against my wife, he brought Theophobus into the palace and kept him with him. But becoming burdened by illness, he shut Theophobus in the chambers of the Bucoleon. When the Persians sought him, asking what had finally become of him, the emperor sent by night Petrona, the brother of the Augusta, along with the logothete, and took off the head of Theophobus. They persuaded the Persians that he was with the emperor in the palace. When the emperor's soul was wretchedly torn away by a dysentery illness, his miserable funeral was carried out to the Church of the Holy Apostles. But the body of Theophobus, having been taken out secretly through the Bucoleon, was safely delivered near the house of Narses, in the place now called "of Theophobus," and they buried it there. C
THE REIGN OF MICHAEL WITH THEODORA HIS MOTHER
1. In the year of the world 6339, and from the divine incarnation 839, Michael, the son of Theophilus, reigned with his mother Theodora for fifteen years, alone for ten years, and with Basil for one year. Having succeeded to the paternal empire, he shook off most sincerely the new religion and superstition that had crept into the Church, hated by God, and had raged terribly against it, and preached the holy, long-established, and most correct faith of God. Theodora was so faithful and orthodox that, while her husband was still living, she secretly honored and venerated the holy icons. By her own will, but also by the suggestion and admonition of Theoctistus, the caniclius and logothete, she expelled the patriarch John, with his followers, from the Church and the city, relegating him to Stenum, to the place called Clidium. It is said that he was also her godfather, the one who had lifted her son from the sacred font. She introduced the saintly monk Methodius, and having restored the Church, established him as patriarch; gathering together all the monks and bishops who had been relegated to exile by Theophilus, she confirmed the orthodox faith and composed the Church in peace on the first Sunday of the Holy Fasts. For although the emperor was an infant and a boy, yet He who *perfected praise out of the mouth of infants and sucklings* (Ps. VIII, 3) also prepared him to celebrate and manifest the truth of the divine dogma.
Krum, having followed after him, besieged the city. Struck by a lance from Leo the Armenian, he returned to Bulgaria and sent [men] to Saint Mamas to remove the bronze statues that were there. Having gone also to the city of Adrianople, he took it and transferred ten thousand men, not counting the women, and settled them beyond the Danube. In the days of the Emperor Theophilus, there was a master of the soldiers in Macedonia named Cordules. He also had a son, Bardas by name, a man of very great fortitude and vigor, whom he left in his stead to rule over the Macedonians who were positioned beyond the river Danube. By means of a certain art and device, he gained access to Theophilus, exactly as has been written above. Having received him most gladly and learned his intention, [Theophilus] sent ships to pick them up and bring them to the city. The prince of Bulgaria was Baldimer, grandson of Krum, the father of Symeon, who later gained power.
9. The people, therefore, took counsel with their wives and children to depart into the Roman territory. When Michael the Bulgarian had gone out into Macedonia, they began to cross over with their belongings. The count, learning of this, crossed over as well to wage war against them. Anxious for this reason, the Macedonians appointed Tzantzes and Cordules as their leaders; and joining battle, they caused a great slaughter of the enemy: some were also taken prisoner. When they were unable to cross over into Bulgaria, they flocked to the Hungarians and told them of their entire situation and that of the Macedonians. Ships sent by the emperor to take them into the city also arrived. Suddenly, a vast multitude of Huns appeared. Seeing them, the [Macedonians] cried out with tears, saying: "God of Saint Adrian, help us," and they drew up in order for battle. The Turks said to them: "Give us all your substance and depart wherever you wish." However, they could not prevail upon their minds to do this, but for three days they stood under arms with their line drawn up; on the fourth day, they began to embark into the ships. Seeing this, the Turks joined battle from the seventh hour until evening; the nation was routed, and the Macedonians pursued them. On the following day, as they wished to withdraw, the Huns appeared again to fight them. Then a younger Macedonian named Leo, of the family of the Gomostoi, who later became the leader of the etaireia (the foreign guard), and other notable men of the Macedonians, rose up, routed and drove them off; and having returned, they boarded the ships and were saved, returning to the emperor. Having been magnificently received by him, they returned to Macedonia, their own country. Basil was at that time about twenty-five years old. Upon returning to his native land, he attached himself to the service of the governor of Macedonia named Tzantzes, to be at his beck and call. Having obtained no benefit from him, he set out for the city as far as the Golden Gate. Having entered, weary from the journey (for it was the Lord's day, and the sun was near setting, he lay down in the...
and also to Theophanes the [p. 533] protovestiarius, as a very brave man surnamed Pharganus, they entered into a conspiracy with Damianus to kill Theoctistus, who was commonly called the prefect of the canicleion. The emperor, too, being induced by Damianus to this opinion, agreed and himself consented that this should happen. For Bardas used to say to Damianus: "As long as Theoctistus is with the Augusta, the emperor will never rule or exercise authority." Theoctistus, having gone to the baths of Areobindus, as was his custom, and then coming to the secretariorum (the court of clerks), entered the Lausiacus holding the petitions and suggestions. And looking around, he saw Bardas sitting with arrogance, and being greatly disturbed, he said, "If I once enter to the Augusta, I will drive him out again and cast him away." But as he was entering the Horologion, Michael met him with Damianus, and they did not allow him to enter to Theodora, but he was ordered with anger to open the petitions and read them before him. And when he had done this against his will, he was compelled to turn back and exit. And he went out weeping bitterly, lamenting, and crying out. Bardas met him in the Lausiacus and began to strike him upon the cheeks and to pluck out his hair. But the drungarius of the watch, Maniaces, seeing this, arose and contradicted Bardas, saying, "Do not strike the logothete." But they slaughtered him and cut him into pieces at the place of the Scytha, showing the cruelty and ferocity of wild beasts.
13. The story of Theodora's grief and the expulsion of her daughters
13. When Theodora learned this from the Pope, she went out against Michael and, as was natural, was indignant, as she was against the others who had perpetrated the murder. And although the emperor moved every stone to appease his mother, she admitted no relief, nor did she abate her wrath, nor was she capable of any consolation; but like a sea wave, she became no gentler nor did she submit. Therefore, just as he changed his mind, judgment, and will, so also the emperor changed his person and his activity, and he strove to afflict her and trouble her in as many ways as he had previously pretended to soothe and cherish her. And so he cast his sisters Thecla, Anastasia, and Anna out of the palace; but Pulcheria, as being beloved by her mother, he sent to the monastery called Gastria. And shortly after, gathering them all together, he caused them to be clothed in the monastic habit in the monastery of Gastria and tonsured their hair.
14. Praised by the Senate, he alone holds imperial power, and he appoints Bardas magister and domestic of the schools. But since his mother was not reconciled because of the things he caused her to suffer, and because she was wounded in spirit by the unjust murder of Theoctistus, an implacable enmity existed between them. And so, driving her from the palace, he sends her away to the monastery of Gastria. She, being overwhelmed in mind by excessive grief and, because of the shock, [deprived of] sound reason...
their own, and having dipped their pens in the precious blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Emperor and Basil subscribed the venerable crosses, confirming with an oath that the Caesar should set out with them on the expedition without any fear. For Leo the Philosopher had openly warned Bardas not to set out with them, saying that if he did, he would not return.
27. Once the feast of Easter was over, the Emperor departed with a large army and entered the theme of the Thracesians. When they had pitched camp at Kepoi, Basil the paracomoimomenos began to plot to put the Caesar to death. In this conspiracy were Maurianus, Symbatius, Asylæon his cousin, Peter the Bulgarian, John the Chaldian, and Constantine Toxaras. But John the Neatocomete learned of this, and went down to the Caesar's tent at sunset and spoke to Procopius, the Caesar's protovestiarius, affirming that their lord the Caesar would be cut to pieces on the morrow. Upon hearing this, the Caesar said to Procopius: "Go and tell the Neatocomete: 'You are raving. You know that you are young, and the dignity of a patrician does not befit you; this is why you are sowing these tares.'" The Caesar, having spent the night without sleep, summoned all his men at the crack of dawn and disclosed to them what had been said to him, asking what they thought was to be done. Philotheus the protospatharios and general, a man very close to him, said to the Caesar: "Tomorrow, master, clothe yourself in your golden, cassia-colored chlamys and reveal yourself before your enemies, and they will flee from your face."
28. When the sun had risen, the Caesar mounted his horse and arrived at the Emperor’s tent in brilliant attire, with many surrounding him, having the energetic protostrator Argyros as his armiger. Constantine Toxaras, on Basil's orders, went to meet him under the guise of paying respect, and returning, announced the Caesar's arrival to Basil. Basil went out, greeted him with reverence, and taking him by the hand, led him to the Emperor. The Caesar, sitting down with the Emperor, said: "Now that all the forces are gathered, master, give the order to cross over to Crete." Basil, standing behind him, was brandishing threats at the Caesar with his hand. The Caesar suddenly turned and saw Basil threatening him, and immediately Basil struck him with his sword, and those who were his companions cut him to pieces, while the Emperor looked on and remained silent.
29. It was the third hour of the day. The Emperor and Basil returned at once to the city. As they were proceeding along the road toward the emporium of Acritos, with a great multitude of people gathered to see the Emperor, a certain man clothed in the monastic habit, standing upon a high rock, shouted in a loud voice to the Emperor: "A magnificent expedition you have performed, O Emperor, having slain your own kinsman and one of your own blood with the sword. Woe to you, woe to you for having done these things!" Driven by rage D ...
only accompanying Basil with favorable omens and disowning him. When the emperors learned of this, they gave orders to the remaining generals and tribunes to advance upon them with hostile standards. But Nicephorus Maleinos, concocting a plan, sent missives among the soldiers so that they might capture them by guile, and not fight in open warfare and fall into civil war. And all abandoned them. Peganes was captured, and they brought him into the city. By order of the emperor, Constantine Myares the Eparch put out his eyes. Having placed him in the Milion, they put a vessel in his hands, and those passing by would throw into it whatever they were inclined to give. And thirty days later, Symbatios the Armenian was captured by Maleinos in a hostel in Celtzine, and the said Maleinos brought him to the emperor at Saint Mamas. And by order of the emperor, they led Peganes out to meet Symbatios, giving into the hands of Peganes an earthenware incense-burner with burning incense, so that he might smoke him out. And they blinded one of the eyes of Symbatios, and cut off his right hand, and seated him at the Lausiacus, and they placed a vessel in his bosom so that whoever had the desire might throw something into it. And after three days they led them away to their own houses, keeping them under guard.
32. Now the emperor Michael, having sent for the mechanical engineer called Damarios, exhumed Constantine Caballinos from the tomb, whom he found whole. And wishing to put him in a sack, and since he would not fit, he wrapped him [in the sack]. Likewise, he also raised the patriarch John from the tomb along with his omophorion: such is the nature of a pontifical garment. And by order of the emperor, the Eparch shut them both in the praetorium, and on the day of the chariot races, having brought them out and stripped them, he beat them with lashes, and having sent their bones to the Amastrianum, he burned them. He cut the urn of Copronymus, which was of green marble and marvelous, with a saw to make pillars in the church he built at the Pharos.
33. The emperor Leo was born of Michael and Eudocia Ingerina, while Michael was still alive, on the first of September, in the fifteenth indiction. The emperor also held chariot races at Saint Mamas, and he himself drove in the first race as a Blue. Constantine the Armenian, the father of Thomas the patrician and Drungary of the Watch, drove as a White; Agalianos drove as a Green, and Chrysas the Russian as a Red. When the emperor emerged victorious and was seated at dinner with Basil and Eudocia, the patrician Basiliscianus was praising the emperor for how skillfully and brilliantly he had driven the chariot. The emperor ordered him to rise, and to take off his red shoes so that he could take them and put them on himself. When the man refused to perform this, and kept looking toward Basil, the emperor in his wrath ordered him to do it. And when Basil did not assent, he put the red boots on the man himself. And the emperor said with an oath to Basil: "They fit him better than they do you. For do I not..."
and scorched the dissevered corpses at the Amastrianum. The deserter from the Persian [army], having become infested with worms, departed from life. Constantine Toxaras perished, cut to pieces by swords, among the Cibyrrhaeots. And Marcian, the brother of the emperor, died after his foot was crushed and he was consumed by worms.
[P. 544] 3. The emperor, proceeding on the day of the Nativity of Christ in procession to the Great Church, baptized his son Stephen in the sacred font. Having brought up white horses with chariots, he sat together with the Augusta. The praepositus Baanes also sat with them, carrying the infant as far as the palace, while the emperor scattered consular largesse along the way.
4. On the feast of St. Polyeuctus, there occurred a great earthquake, so that the earth shook for a space of forty days. At that time the globe of the statue in the Forum fell, and also the church of the most holy Theotokos, which is called the Sigma, was destroyed, so that all who were chanting divine praises there perished. Leo the Philosopher, who happened to be there, told the chanters and all who were present there to leave the church; but they, not obeying, were all destroyed in the ruin. The philosopher himself, having stood by a column under the timber supports, escaped safe with two others, and only five others [survived] situated under the ambo.
5. Moreover, Photius the patriarch, when the emperor had come into the church and was waiting to receive the sacred mysteries, called him a brigand and a parricide, and [said he was] unworthy of the divine communion. The emperor, enraged, sent to Rome and brought back a tome with Latin bishops, and expelled Photius from the throne, and again promoted the holy Ignatius to the priesthood. Alexander the emperor was born of Eudocia, daughter of Inger; he was the legitimate son of Basil.
6. The emperor campaigned against the Agarenes who were in Tephrica, and having joined battle, he was defeated; and not rarely, when battle was joined with the Agarenes, no small slaughter [of forces] was inflicted upon the Romans. In such a flight of the emperor, Theophylactus Abastactus—the father of Romanus, who later held power—saved the emperor when he had all but been captured and made a prisoner by the Agarenes. The emperor, having searched for him afterward, and having found and recognized him (for many said to the emperor, "I am the one..."), disregarded the honor [he was offered] and asked for an imperial estate, which he obtained. The emperor, having returned to the city, sent his son-in-law, Christopher, to Tephrica, and having achieved a great victory, he ravaged and destroyed it down to the ground.
7. Ignatius the patriarch built a beautiful church in the emporium of Satyros [P. 545] in the name of the commander of the heavenly host of the East, and he established a monastery for men, where his body also lies.
Moreover, having smashed the great statue of Solomon which was in the basilica, he ordered it to be recast in his own name and placed beneath the foundations of this same New Church, as if he were offering himself as a sacrifice to God by means of such a structure.
15. The emperor campaigned again against Melitene, and having taken captives and brought many wars to a successful conclusion, he returned.
16. Patriarch Ignatius died, and the emperor again raised Photius to the patriarchate in his place for the second time. After this, when Patriarch Photius had died in exile, his body was deposited in the monastery called that of Jeremiah in Mardosagari, which had previously been a catholic church and a parish serving both sexes; but Photius had made it a convent for women.
17. Again the emperor went on an expedition into Germanicia, and having taken it and brought it under the yoke, he returned.
18. Constantine, the son of the emperor Michael and Eudocia, died—though rumor among the people says he was the son of Basil. Basil mourned him greatly, since he loved him much. His body was deposited in the tombs of the emperors.
19. On the first of May, the New Church, which the emperor had built and adorned with great beauty, was dedicated and opened for public divine worship by Patriarch Photius; the emperor wore the loros, gave much money, and gave it the name "New."
20. Procopius, the protovestiarius, was sent by the emperor with all the western themes; Eupraxius, the master of soldiers, was in Sicily; Musulices in Cephalonia; Rabduchus in Dyrrhachium; and Oniatus and Apostyppus in the Peloponnese. This Procopius, having performed many deeds and acts of valor, was killed in the final battle, having been betrayed by Apostyppus.
21. Leo the Salibaras joined Theodore, a monk and archbishop of Euchaita, with Patriarch Photius, describing him as pious, a wonder-worker, and endowed with the gift of foreknowledge. Photius introduced him to the emperor and brought them together. The emperor was pleased with him—for he was disposed according to his desires—and therefore held him in great honor. When the emperor was despondent over the death of his son Constantine, due to the love he bore for him, this man deceived him and promised to show him the boy alive; and he did so. For when the emperor was passing through a certain thicket, a mounted phantom met him, dressed in gold-woven garments, appearing in the likeness of Constantine. The emperor saw him with his own eyes, and having embraced him, he kissed him. When the vision vanished, he believed he had truly seen him, and was not being deceived. Therefore, having built a monastery there, he named it after Saint Constantine.
under the emperors Constantine and his mother Zoe.
7-8. During the reign of Leo, Hagius, the exarch and duke of Longobardia, and son-in-law of the king of the Franks, having stirred up a rebellion against the emperor, subjugated the whole region to his own authority. When the emperor learned of this, he sent Constantine, the master of the table, with all the western legions to wage war against Hagius. A battle was joined, and Constantine’s army was defeated and slaughtered, he himself barely escaping with his life.
9. There was an eclipse of the sun, so that it became night at the sixth hour and the stars appeared. Moreover, there were thunderclaps, windstorms, and lightning, such that seven men were consumed by fire on the steps of the Forum.
10. The fortress of Samos was besieged by the Agarenes, and their general Paspalas was taken prisoner. Emperor Leo appointed Zautzas as basileopator, having already become reconciled with his daughter Zoe, after her husband Theodore Gauaniatus had died by some poison. And when Stephen the patriarch had departed this life, Antony, called Cauleas, was ordained in his place.
11. A messenger also came from the praetor of Macedonia, saying that Symeon, the prince of the Bulgarians, intended to campaign against the Romans. The cause of Symeon’s anger was this: Zautzas the basileopator had a slave eunuch named Musicus. He had become friends with Stauracius and Cosmas, Helladics who were greedy for gold and merchants, who, for the sake of shameful gain and desiring to increase their wealth by any means, through the intercession and influence of Musicus, who was in the city, diverted the trade of the Bulgarians from the city to Thessalonica, dealing unfairly with the Bulgarians in matters of commerce. The Bulgarians reported this to Symeon. He made these things known to Emperor Leo. But the emperor, hindered by the basileopator, who was favorably disposed toward Musicus, heard it all as if it were idle nonsense.
12. The Bulgarian, then, being enraged, campaigned against the Romans. Learning of this, the emperor sent the magistros of the military, Crenetes, against the Bulgarians with arms and many of the city’s nobles and officials, to move against Symeon. A battle was joined in Macedonia, and the Romans were routed, with both Crenetes and the Armenian Curticius, as well as all the rest, being slaughtered. Then, some of the Chazars, who were serving in the company of Leo, were captured; and their noses having been cut off as a disgrace to the Romans, they were sent to the city by Symeon. Seeing them, the emperor was filled with rage and sent Nicetas, surnamed Sclerus, with triremes into the river Danube to offer gifts to the Turks and to stir up war
Zoe, his daughter; for Theophano, his wife, was not there, but was at the Blachernae, assiduously present at the holy shrine. A Since it seemed good to the emperor to remain there, the kinsmen and relatives of Zautza—namely his son Tzaoutzes and the others—having held a council, conspired to kill the emperor by night. But Zoe, sleeping in the bosom of the emperor, heard the tumult, and looking through the window, she signaled for silence. When she perceived the danger of the conspiracy they had plotted, she awakened the emperor. He immediately boarded a boat and crossed over to Pegas, leaving Zautza and the rest there, and at dawn he entered the palace in haste. He expelled John, the drungarios of the watch, and appointed Pardos, the son of Nicolaus, as count of the *foederati* in his place; for Nicolaus was bound in friendship to the emperor and made known to him everything concerning Zautza, until Leo, the *magister* known as Theodotakes, reconciled them.
17. Theophano Augusta died, having reigned for twelve years. Not many days later, God revealed her as a worker of miracles, because she was free from jealousy and because she was assiduous in alms and in prayers unceasingly to God and the holy churches.
18. The emperor Leo crowned Zoe, the daughter of Zautza. Because the intimacy of Zoe with the emperor while Theophano was still alive had been condemned, the marriage was not blessed by the patriarch, but was considered illicit. And the priest who blessed it, even though he was acting on behalf of the emperor, was nonetheless deposed by decree of the synod. And Leo was blessed with her by a palace cleric, surnamed Sinapes. And the one who blessed them was indeed removed from his rank. Zoe, however, reigned for one year and eight months. When she died, they found a sarcophagus in which to lay her body, having words engraved within it that read thus: *Daughter of Babylon, wretched one.*
19. Musicus and Staurakios were accused before the emperor Leo of receiving gifts for their intercession with the emperor’s father on behalf of the generals and officials and nobles. And once, when Staurakios had entered to see Zautza with letters from one of the generals, the emperor saw him standing in the solarium. He came up behind him, grabbed him by the nape of the neck, and dragged him out, as if to question him regarding the generals. And having led him to the Monothyron, and having taken the letters and thrown them away, he handed him over to those present to be taken out of the palace, ordering that he have his hair shorn. When Musicus heard this, he fell into despair and his spirit collapsed. The emperor, coming to the place where Musicus stood by Zautza, pushed him by the neck and cast him out, handing him over to Christophoros the *koubikoularios* to be taken to the Studion and made a monk. Not long after, Zautza died.
of him Basilius, the count of the foederati, the Camateros, and George the Crentas, in order to apprehend him. And when Samonas wished to cross the Halys, Nicephorus the drungarios, who was called Caminas, overtook him and prevented him from crossing. And since Samonas promised much but did not persuade him, he took refuge in the venerable cross which is at Siricha, pretending that he had made his journey for the sake of faith in the cross. Constantinus, son of Ducas, therefore apprehended him, and taking him back, returned to the city. The emperor ordered that he be guarded in the house of Bardas Caesar, and he inquired of Constantinus, son of Ducas, about him. Having learned that he was truly fleeing to Syria, he charged the son of Ducas not to say this in the presence of the senate, when asked by him, but that he had gone to Siricha for the sake of a vow; for the emperor wished to admit him into his presence. Having therefore summoned Constantinus, son of Ducas, before those in power, he said: "As you hold God and my head, was Samonas fleeing or not?"
