He, however, as one who had previously asked the emperor not to swear oaths and [not] to command him to say what he was told, upon hearing the oaths and the religious import of these movements, announced before all: "Certainly, he was fleeing to Syria." The emperor sent him away in anger, and kept Samonas for four months in the house of Bardas. And he reconciled with him in the emperor's sovereignty. [P. 559] 32. At that time, a comet star also appeared, sending its rays toward the East, and it was seen for forty days and as many nights. Samonas was created a patrician from the time of his flight. Leo begat a son from Zoe, his fourth wife; and he was baptized on the day of the Lights by the patriarch Nicholas in the Great Church, with the emperor Alexander, the patrician Samonas, and the whole senate as his godparents. 33. Then the Cypha was made a hospital for the care of the elderly, with the prostitutes cast out. 34. The emperor Leo was wedded with nuptial blessing to Zoe after the feast of the Lord's birth by Thomas the presbyter, who was deposed. He also proclaimed the same Zoe Augusta; and for this reason the patriarch forbade the emperor from entering the church. Wherefore he passed on the right side as far as the metatorium, not at all walking through in the customary manner. 35. Samonas was promoted to praepositus of the sacred bedchamber, because he was the emperor's assistant and associate in every crime and wickedness. And they began to plot against the Church. For having summoned the patriarch Nicholas in the month of February, and having pleaded much that polygamy be received, when they were unable to bring him to their opinion, they embarked him from the Cletorion via the Bucoleon in a small boat and ferried him across to the
A, terrifying them. From that time on, the peace being broken, Symeon prepared to move his arms against the Christians.
7. Now Alexander, having breakfasted and having been struck by a divine sword, passed away, with much blood flowing from his nostrils and private parts; after two days he died, leaving as guardians the Patriarch Nicholas, Stephen the *magistros*, and John Eladas, as well as John the *rhaiktor*, Euthymius, Basilitzes, and Gabrielopoulos, having left the empire to his nephew Constantine, son of the Emperor Leo. Alexander was buried in the tombs with his father.
THE REIGN OF CONSTANTINE, SON OF LEO
1. Constantine, after his father had passed away, while still a boy (for he was in his seventh year of age), had been left in the empire by his uncle Alexander under guardians. He reigned, therefore, under guardians with his mother for another seven years; and with Romanus, his father-in-law, to whom he was subject, for another twenty-six years; and finally, he ruled the empire alone for fifteen years, so that the entire time of his reign is comprised in the space of fifty-five years. Since the Patriarch Nicholas had seized the power of the palace, as he himself was a guardian along with the *magistros* Stephen and John Eladas—who was likewise a *magistros*—he took charge of public affairs and diligently devoted himself daily to matters pertaining to the empire.
2. While the affairs of the empire were thus situated, it was suggested to Constantine Ducas, who was *domestikos* of the schools*, by certain of the magnates in the royal city who loved him as a brave and prudent man and one who could well govern the empire, that he should enter the city and take control of it without any labor, transferring the summit of power to himself. He, for his part, as someone who had long dreamed of the empire and was always consumed with the desire for the crown and diadem, flew into the city with all possible haste with a chosen band of his men, and a multitude also not to be despised. At night, having entered through a postern gate belonging to the *protovestiarios* Michael, not far from the acropolis and by an obscure entrance, he spent the night sleeplessly with his companions in the house of his father-in-law, Gregoras. But Nicetas, a secretary—who later became *protonotarios*—announced Constantine’s arrival to the patrician Constantine and the monk Eladikos; and both of them came that same night to Constantine Ducas. Having taken counsel, and while it was not yet day but still dark, they invaded the gate of the Circus with torches and a not insignificant armed force of soldiers and hailed Constantine as emperor with auspicious acclamations. There, his equerry was pierced by a lance by the guards and those who were inside the gates, and fell dead.
3. Constantine, therefore, not being accepted—nay, being repelled—as if driven by the frenzy of some demon, and not being in possession of his senses due to his love of the empire, withdrew into the Circus, sad and with downcast face, considering the death of his equerry to be a bad omen.
having received the peace most willingly, sent Theodore the magister to discuss the terms of peace. A Patriarch Nicholas, along with Stephen and John the magistri, having taken the emperor with them, went as far as the Blachernae, and brought in the two sons of Symeon, and they were entertained at a feast by the emperor in the palace. Patriarch Nicholas went out to Symeon, to whom Symeon bowed his head modestly. The patriarch, therefore, having said a prayer over him, placed his own head covering upon his head in place of a crown, as they say. Consequently, Symeon and his sons, having been presented with boundless and most generous gifts, returned to their own land, having departed without sufficient agreement on the aforementioned peace.
8. Since the emperor Constantine was a child and was constantly asking for his mother—for Alexander the emperor, as has been said, had already cast her out of the palace—they brought her back again into the palace. Having therefore obtained power and taken hold of the administration of civil affairs, she brought into the palace Constantine the parakoimomenos, and Constantine and Anastasius, called the Gongylioi, and, on the advice of John Eladas, she removed the associates of the emperor Alexander—John the raiktor, and him who is called Gabrielopoulos, and Basilitzes, and the rest. Zoe Augusta appointed Theophylact Domenicus as hetaireiarches. But John Eladas the magister, having fallen ill and being given up by his doctors, left the palace and, having arrived at the Blachernae and being weighed down by the illness, he died. On the advice of the hetaireiarches Domenicus, Zoe cast out Patriarch Nicholas along with his attendants in anger, telling him to care for his own church.
9. Not long after, Constantine the parakoimomenos accused the hetaireiarches Domenicus to the Augusta, as if he were plotting to transfer supreme power to his brother. Decorating him therefore with the dignity of a patrician as a pretext, when he had gone down to the church to receive a blessing according to the custom, they ordered him to stay at home. Zoe appointed in his place John Garidas as hetaireiarches, and she created the eunuch Damian as drungarios of the vigla. Domenicus went off to his own house lamenting in vain.
10. But because the Bulgar Symeon was again plundering Thrace, and because the Augusta and the officials were anxious about how they might at last put a stop to the man’s arrogance, John the Bogas asked to become a patrician, promising to bring the Patzinaks against him. Having obtained his request and received gifts, he went off to the land of the Patzinaks, and having indeed made a treaty and received hostages from there, he brought them into the city, as the Patzinaks had agreed to cross the Ister and to wage war upon Symeon.
11. There arrived also in the city Asotios, a man most renowned for his strength, being the son of the prince of the princes of
they agreed to contract, thereby tightening the bond of love all the more.
25. Therefore, on the twenty-fourth day of the month of March, Romanos sent John the presbyter, who was his own household member and most trusted man, and Theodore, called Mastzuces, into the palace to defend his cause and excuse him, saying that what he had done did not look toward rebellion; but that, being suspicious of Phocas’s aggression and fearing that he might plot some innovation against the emperor, he had for this reason resolved to come up into the palace and take over the guardianship of the emperor. Since Nicholas the patriarch did not approve of or agree to these things, it was signified to Romanos by the aforementioned tutor that he should come with his entire fleet as far as Bucoleon. B Having consulted with those who were with him (for the driving force was leading him), on the very day of the Annunciation of the all-holy Theotokos (which was a Thursday), he came armed with his entire fleet to Bucoleon. And immediately, Stephen the magister went out of the palace, and Nicetas the patrician, the father-in-law of Romanos, went up into the palace and expelled Nicholas the patriarch from it. Therefore, since the spirits of the courtiers had been fortified for Romanos by oath, they sent the precious cross to him; and having venerated it and bound himself by oath to them again, he went up with them and a few of his company to the palace to greet the emperor. Having entered with him into the church that is in the Pharos, and having given and received pledges of security there, he was immediately appointed by him as magister and grand hetaireiarches.
26. Immediately, therefore, a divine command was issued to Leo Phocas, instructing him in no way to conceive of any sedition, but to rest for a short time in his own house. Likewise, letters were ordered to be written to Constantine the parakoimomenos, commanding him the same things, not to plot anything contrary, but to remain in subjection to the emperor Constantine. Andreas, primikirios of the imperial vestiarion, took these and delivered them to him in Cappadocia; and having received and read them, he returned home and remained quiet in his own house.
27. In the fifth week of the holy Fasts, the engagement of the marriage contract is given by the emperor Constantine to Helena, the daughter of Romanos, and on the third day of Easter, which is called the [day] of Galilee, she is blessed and crowned together with him by the patriarch Nicholas.
28. Having appointed Romanos basileopator, he set up his son, Christopher, in his stead as hetaireiarches. Not much time had passed, however, when Leo Phocas, having been deceived by certain nobles and his own tribunes and at the urging of the legions subject to him, began to plot new things; and having summoned Constantine the accubitor, and Constantine and Anastasius the Gongylioi,
having done this by design, so that Romanos, the father of the emperor, was indignant at this matter.
31. In the month of August, the plot of Constantine Ktematikos, David Kamulianos, and Michael, curator of the Manganon, was denounced. Being beaten and having their goods confiscated, they were led through the middle of the city and sent away into exile. Leo, the magister and domestic of the schools, was also brought into the city and led through the middle of the forum sitting on a mule for the sake of ignominy.
32-33. Moreover, Zoe Augusta was caught plotting against Romanos by means of poisoned food prepared by Theokletos, a notary of the service. On account of this, they cast her out of the palace and led her away to the Petrion, tonsuring her in the monastery of Saint Euphemia.
34. Furthermore, Theophylaktos the patrician and count of the stable invited to a meal Theodore, the pedagogue of the emperor Constantine, and his brother Symeon. Then John the droungarios of the watch, called Kourkouas, entered with a great retinue, and seizing them as though they were conspiring against Romanos, he exiled them to the Opsikion in their own suburban estates.
35. On the twenty-fourth of the month of September, Romanos was gifted with the dignity of Caesar; and in the month of December, on the seventeenth day, the Sunday of the Fathers, which is before the Nativity, he was gifted with the imperial diadem by the emperor Constantine, his son-in-law, and the patriarch Nicholas. And on the sixth of January, which is the holy day of the Lights, he crowned Theodora, his wife, with the imperial crown. In the month of May, on the seventeenth, in the fifth indiction, Christophorus, the son of Romanos, was proclaimed emperor, and was crowned by the emperor Constantine on the twenty-fourth of the same month, on the day of Holy Pentecost; and these two alone marched in that procession.
1-2. In the month of June, in the eighth indiction, on a Sunday, the union of the Church was made through the agency of Romanos, with all being united, both metropolitans and clerics, who had been divided by the schism of Nicholas and Euthymios.
3. On the eighth of the month of February, in the ninth indiction, Romanos sent away and exiled to the island of Antigonis, Stephen, the son of Calomaria, as one accused of aspiring to the empire, and tonsured him a monk together with Theophanes Tichiotis and Paul the Orphanotrophos, his domestic servants.
4. The emperor Romanos held a silentium in the tribunal, with all the cohorts gathered there in arms. But when the announcement was made, suddenly Romanos and Constantine returned with great speed to the palace; for information had been brought by Leo, the servant of Arsenios, that Arsenios himself and Paul the manglabites were weaving a plot; wherefore, being beaten and having their goods confiscated, they were relegated to exile. At that time, the presbyter John, the rector, was the assistant in the administration of the commonwealth and his intimate minister. He
lower, marshy places of Pegae, the Bulgarians appeared from above, armed, and using a signal that was unclear, fearful, and extremely violent, they charged against them. Joannes the rector immediately took to flight, but Photinus, son of Platypus, fighting in his defense, was killed, as were many others. With difficulty, therefore, he escaped and got into a dromon. Alexius the drungary, the Musele, also came fleeing, armed; and not being able to climb the ladder of the dromon, he fell into the sea with his protomandator and was drowned. Both of the Argyroi escaped by flight into the fort. The rest of the naval and all other forces, some fleeing the hands of the enemy, were drowned in the sea; others perished by the sword; and others were taken captive by Bulgarian hands. The Bulgarians, no one resisting them, then burned the royal buildings of Pegae and laid waste the entire Stenos with fire. Such, indeed, is the great calamity caused by lack of counsel and inexperience in affairs, to which boldness and temerity act as assistants.
10. In the month of February, in the tenth indiction, Theodora, wife of Romanus, died; her body was deposited in the house of the emperor Romanus himself, which he had converted into a monastery.
11. In the same month, Sophia, wife of the emperor Christopher, was crowned. At that same time, the Iberian curopalates arrived in the city, and having been led through the middle of the forum, which had been magnificently adorned, he was received with great pomp and honor. He was also led into the Holy Sophia of God, to inspect its beauty, its scale, and its precious ornamentation; for having adorned it splendidly and draped the walls with gold-woven tapestries and decorated it with all manner of finery, they thus admitted him. He, astonished at the wondrous and immense fabric of the temple, and admiring beyond measure its most precious adornment, calling this sacred place the dwelling of God, returned to his home.
12. But in the month of June, the Bulgarians campaigned again, and having reached the royal residences of Saint Theodora and having given them over to fire, the emperor Romanus invited the commanders and tribunes of the regiments to a banquet; among them was he who is called Sactices; and he exhorted and urged them to go out against the adversaries and fight for the fatherland. They agreed readily to die for his majesty and the safety of the Christian cause. On the next day, therefore, the aforementioned Sactices, having been armed by the emperor, demonstrated the true faith he had given and the virtue of his brave soul; for coming upon the Bulgarians from the rear and falling upon their camp, he slaughtered all those whom he found there. When the Bulgarians learned what had occurred, they returned to the camp, and a battle having been joined, they compelled Sactices, being accompanied by few, to turn his back. Since he
Patriarch Stephen, along with Theophanes the protovestiarios and Maria, daughter of Christopher, and the entire senate, went to the church of the most holy Theotokos at the Pegai, where they blessed Peter and Maria and placed the nuptial crowns upon their heads, with Theophanes the protovestiarios and George Sursubulos acting as groomsmen. A sumptuous and most magnificent banquet having been prepared, and all things pertaining to the nuptial rites having been performed fittingly and splendidly, Theophanes the protovestiarios returned to the city along with Maria, the daughter of Emperor Christopher. On the third day after the celebration of the marriage, Emperor Romanus prepared a magnificent feast at the landing place of the Pegai, having adorned the structure with silk hangings. In this place, Emperor Romanus feasted with Peter the Bulgarian, as well as with his son-in-law Constantine and his son Christopher. When the Bulgarians, with firm resolve, insisted that Christopher be acclaimed first, and then Constantine, with favorable omens and shouts in the manner of emperors, Emperor Romanus yielded to their insistence, and that which they requested came to pass. 33-34. When all the nuptial solemnities and joys were completed, and Maria was now about to set out for Bulgaria with her husband Peter, her parents accompanied her as far as the Hebdomon, together with Theophanes the protovestiarios. And after having feasted there with Peter, since the time for departure was at hand, they embraced their daughter, shedding many tears—as was fitting for those who were about to be deprived of a most beloved child—and after kissing their son-in-law and placing their daughter into his own hands, they returned to the palace. Maria, delivered into the hands of her Bulgarian husband, made the journey to Bulgaria with joy mixed with sadness: sadness for the fact that she was deprived of her most loving parents, of the royal palaces, and of the habit of those who were kin to her family; and joy because she had been joined to a king as a husband and was hailed as mistress of the Bulgarians. She departed, therefore, taking with her splendid riches of every kind and an uncountable supply of furnishings. 35. At that time, envoys also came from Melitene to Emperor Romanus, offering to establish covenants of peace. Not long after, Apochaps, grandson of Amer, himself the amir of Melitene, and Aposalath the commander—a very noble, wealthy, and vigorous man—also came from Melitene; and having been received with magnificent honor and the proper respect by Romanus, and having struck a peace agreement, they returned to their own lands. From then on, they were numbered as allies and joined in military campaigns with the Romans against their own kin, the Hagarenes, and they would enter the city with the Romans during the triumphal processions, bringing with them Hagarene captives—a truly wonderful and paradoxical indication of the downfall of the godless Hagarenes. But after the death of Apochaps, a prudent, wise, and powerful man, those who inhabited Melitene broke the peace. Consequently, the Domestic of the Schools, John Curcuas, once again campaigns against them with his cohorts
and legions, and Melias, the commander of the Armenians. These, making many forays every day and devastating the whole region far and wide with constant incursions, and leading away captives, drove Melitene into such straits that in a very short time they conquered it and razed it to the ground. And not only it, but also the cities and regions adjacent to it, which were fertile, most rich, and afforded many other revenues. The emperor, therefore, reducing this Melitene into a curatorship (others call it a province), extracted many thousands of gold and silver pieces from it.
36. Nicetas, Magister and father-in-law of the Emperor Christopher, was accused of having instigated him against his own father and having been the author of his dethronement. Wherefore, having cast him out of the city, they tonsured him a monk, condemning him to exile in his own suburb.
37. On the eighteenth day of the month of July, in the sixth indiction, the patriarch Stephen dies, having served for two years and eleven months. But on the fourteenth day of the month of December, they bring in Tryphon, a monk dwelling in the Opsikion, a man testified to for his religious character and holiness, and they ordain him patriarch for a fixed time, until Theophylact, the son of the Emperor Romanus, should reach the measure of age whom they intended to make patriarch of Constantinople.
38. On the twenty-fifth day of the same month, an unbearable winter occurred, so that the earth was frozen into ice for 120 days. Whence there also came a great famine, surpassing any that had ever existed, and such a great number of deaths followed from it, that the living were not able to carry out the dead. The Emperor Romanus, considering that violence of such great evil, which no power of words could explain, took thought worthy of his compassionate and merciful nature, alleviating the want of the famine with generous alms. He closed up the spaces of the porticoes with windows and wooden boards, so that the snow and the cold might not penetrate from there to the poor. Then he also constructed what are called arculae (chests) in all the porticoes, and ordered that a silver coin be given every month to the poor lying there; he also ordered monthly trimisia to be distributed to the poor through the churches, [totaling] twelve thousand pieces of stamped silver. And his mind, inclined to mercy and a generous provider for the poor, did not only establish these things, but he also ordered that three poor men eat with him every day, who each received one coin as an alms. On Wednesday and Friday, he would admit three poor monks as companions at the table, who themselves also received a fixed coin each. And that which is observed in the monasteries by legitimate rite, that while eating reading should take place, this he also did himself, providing the table for himself and the guests.
while others they set up like targets and riddled with a shower of javelins. As many of the priestly and clerical order as they managed to seize, they would bind their hands behind their backs and drive iron nails through the middle of their heads. They also surrendered many holy churches to the flames. But as winter was already setting in, and they were running short of provisions, and the army feared both an assault on land and a naval battle, they decided to return home. And indeed, while they were trying to conceal their fleet, in the month of September, in the fifteenth indiction, attempting to sail across toward the Thracian regions, they were met by the patrician Theophanes; for they could not escape his most vigilant and noble spirit. Immediately, therefore, a second naval battle was joined, and the aforementioned man sank very many ships and killed many of them. A few survived with their ships and, having fallen upon the rugged coast of Koile at the onset of night, they escaped. But the patrician Theophanes returned with a brilliant victory and very great trophies, and was received honorably and magnificently, and was honored with the dignity of Parakoimomenos.
52. Since the aforementioned John, the Magister Krokeas, was a man of excellent military skill, and had erected many great trophies, and had widened the Roman borders, and had sacked very many cities of the Agarenes, the Emperor Romanos, because of the brilliance of that man's virtue, desired to take his daughter as a bride for his own grandson, Romanos, who was born to his son Constantine. But, as envy against him was stirred up from this affair by the other emperors, he was cast out of his office, and the patrician Pantherios, a kinsman of the Emperor Romanos, was appointed Domestic in his stead.
53. In the first indiction, in the month of April, the Turks came again with a very large force. But the patrician Theophanes, the Parakoimomenos, going out, made peaceful terms with them, taking hostages from among those of high standing; from which it also came about that peace and a truce were maintained for five years.
54. In the second indiction, the Emperor Romanos sent the protospatharios and strategos of Langobardia, Paschalius, to Hugh, king of Francia, seeking his daughter as a bride for Romanos, the son of his son-in-law Constantine; and having taken her, the aforementioned Paschalius brought her into the city with much luxury and wealth. The wedding took place in the month of September, in the third indiction. She lived with her husband for five years; and she died in the days of the reign of Constantine, her own father-in-law.
55. When a very violent wind had blown in the month of December, those called by the name of the factions in the Hippodrome, 918 who at that time were standing opposite the royal throne, and they crushed…
LIVES OF THE RECENT EMPERORS BY GEORGE THE MONK
those who had not spared their father would certainly not spare themselves. He received them at a banquet, and while they were reclining at the table and the food was still in their mouths, he seized them through those called the Tornikioi, the patrician Marianos, and the rest prepared for this purpose; and having cast them out of the palace, he tonsured them according to the rite of the clergy in the neighboring islands.
3. Not long after, having requested permission to see their own father, they arrived at the island of Prote. Seeing him in monastic habit, they were seized with unbearable grief. The father wept with them and said: "I have begotten and raised sons, but they themselves have abandoned me." Thus they were then relegated: Stephen, indeed, to Proikonnesos, and from Proikonnesos they transferred him to Rhodes, and thence to Mytilene; and Constantine to Tenedos, and thence to Samothrace, where, having plotted a rebellion, he was slain by those who were guarding him. As for Michael, the son of the Emperor Christopher, he took off his imperial buskins and made him a cleric.
4. While the Emperor Romanus was on the island, the Patriarch Theophylact, the patrician Theophanes, and the parakoimomenos held a council to bring him back to the palace. Having communicated this plan to him and persuaded him, they were watching for an opportune time to put an end to this conspiracy. But when the plot was discovered and reported to the Emperor Constantine, he took action against its authors and accomplices. He relegated the patrician Theophanes into exile; but he had the protospatharios and pincernes George, and the primikerios Thomas, scourged and tonsured, and paraded them through the middle of the city, and in this manner sent them into exile.
5. In the month of December, in the sixth indiction, some plotted a conspiracy against the Emperor Constantine, with the intention of bringing the Emperor Stephen from the island where he was staying into the palace. This conspiracy having been reported to Constantine by Michael, called Diabolinos, the Emperor had the conspirators seized; he cut off the noses and ears of some, and subjected others to an intolerable torture of scourging, and having set them upon donkeys, he paraded them through the middle of the city and sent them into exile in disgrace.
9. On the fifteenth day of the month of July, in the sixth indiction, the Emperor Romanus died on the island which they call Prote. His corpse was brought into the city and laid to rest in the monastery he had founded.
[P. 594] Up to this point is the edition of the *Scriptores post Theophanem* by François Combefis.
